> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Basic-Psychological-Needs-—-The-Three|Basic-Psychological-Needs — The Three]]*

> [!definition] **Bayesian Inference** **[perm-note]**
> A framework for updating probability estimates in light of new evidence, governed by Bayes' theorem: posterior probability is proportional to prior probability multiplied by the likelihood of the evidence given the hypothesis. Provides the normative model against which human inductive reasoning is measured in cognitive science. Human reasoners approximate Bayesian inference in many natural settings but systematically violate it in others, particularly when surface-level cognitive heuristics produce systematic biases.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Bayesian-Inference|Bayesian Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Beck's Three-Tier Vocabulary Framework** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A classification of English vocabulary into three pedagogically relevant tiers. *Tier 1* comprises basic, everyday vocabulary acquired through everyday conversation and experience (e.g., *happy*, *walk*, *table*). *Tier 2* comprises high-frequency academic and literary vocabulary that appears across multiple domains of formal discourse but is rarely used in everyday conversation (e.g., *pernicious*, *infer*, *consequence*) — this is the vocabulary most critical for academic literacy and most underserved by reading alone. *Tier 3* comprises low-frequency, domain-specific tech...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Beck's-Three-Tier-Vocabulary-Framework|Beck's Three-Tier Vocabulary Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Belief Bias** **[perm-note]**
> A systematic error in deductive reasoning in which the perceived believability of a conclusion influences judgments of argument validity. Reasoners tend to accept invalid arguments with believable conclusions and reject valid arguments with unbelievable conclusions. Belief bias is explained within dual-process theory as the intrusion of System 1's rapid evaluation of conclusion plausibility into the System 2 task of assessing logical structure. It is among the most robust findings in the psychology of reasoning, observed across cultures and education levels.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Belief-Bias|Belief Bias]]*

> [!definition] **Belonging Uncertainty** **[perm-note]**
> The experience of uncertainty about whether one is valued and accepted in an academic or professional setting — particularly common for students from groups historically underrepresented in certain domains. Belonging uncertainty interacts with mindset: environments that communicate fixed mindset (through selective praise, high-stakes tracking, or responses to failure) tend to heighten belonging uncertainty for already-marginalized students, creating compounding disadvantage.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Belonging-Uncertainty|Belonging Uncertainty]]*

> [!definition] **Bidirectional Enrichment Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> The Bidirectional Enrichment Hypothesis proposes that a PKB designed for schema externalization participates in a bidirectional feedback relationship with the practitioner's internal schematic [[Long-Term-Memory]] organization. The outside-in direction: building a schema-fidelity PKB enriches internal memory organization through self-explanation effects and the generation effect. The inside-out direction: richer internal schemas enable more structurally accurate external schema construction. Both directions together generate a compounding generative cycle over sustained use.
> 
> **Boundary:** ...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[Bidirectional-Enrichment-Hypothesis|Bidirectional Enrichment Hypothesis]]*

> [!definition] **Bipartite Load Model** **[perm-note]**
> The simplified CLT framework, defended by this dialectical re-examination as more theoretically defensible than the tripartite model, that recognizes only two load types: intrinsic (content complexity × learner schemas × goal structure) and extraneous (design-imposed processing inefficiency). Germane processing is retained as a design goal within this model but not as a measurable third load type. Boundary condition: the bipartite model does not imply that all non-extraneous load is beneficial — it creates space for the productive challenge distinction, which the original tripartite model c...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Bipartite-Load-Model|Bipartite Load Model]]*

> [!definition] **The Bootstrapping Constraint** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The structural constraint on schema construction arising from the fact that building new schemas requires existing schemas as constructive inputs. For categorically novel concepts, this creates a circularity: adequate comprehension of the novel concept requires a schema that can only be built by comprehending the novel concept.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** The bootstrapping constraint is most acute for categorical novelty (concepts outside all existing schema categories). It is less acute for degree novelty (difficult instances of familiar categories), which is handled by accom...
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Bootstrapping-Constraint|The Bootstrapping Constraint]]*

> [!definition] **Bridging Inference** **[perm-note]**
> A bridging inference is an inference generated to establish local coherence between adjacent or nearby propositions — to bridge an apparent gap in the text's explicit content that would otherwise produce a coherence failure. When a reader encounters "Mary put the cake in the oven. When the timer rang, she took it out and cut it," the bridging inference BAKED(cake) is required to make the second sentence cohere with the first: without it, the referent of "it" is ambiguous and the causal sequence connecting the two clauses is unclear.
> 
> **Boundary:** Bridging inferences are generated automatic...
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Bridging-Inference|Bridging Inference]]*

## _C

> [!definition] **Calibration** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which an agent's confidence in their beliefs matches the frequency with which such beliefs are true. A well-calibrated agent who expresses 70% confidence in a class of claims is right approximately 70% of the time. Overconfidence — the prevalent human tendency — is the systematic practical violation of fallibilist norms. Calibration research connects epistemology to cognitive psychology and decision science.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Calibration]]*

> [!definition] **Calibration Dependency** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A property of multi-stage feedback systems in which the system's adaptive outputs are constrained by the accuracy of the internal signals flowing between stages. A system exhibits calibration dependency when it can "run" while producing no genuine adaptation because its monitoring signals have become systematically inaccurate. Originally identified in this report as the primary structural vulnerability of the Zimmerman cycle; applicable to any feedback architecture in which signal accuracy is not guaranteed by the system's own structure. An original analytical contribution o...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Calibration-Dependency|Calibration Dependency]]*

> [!definition] **Calibration Engine** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A reframing of Zimmerman's SRL cycle in which the cycle's primary product is not performance on individual learning episodes but the progressive refinement of the learner's internal models of their own capabilities, effective strategies, and task structure. Under this framing, the quality of a learning episode is measured by calibration update accuracy, not performance outcome.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The calibration engine framing resolves the puzzle of why high-achieving students often have poorly developed SRL, and reframes the instructional goal as maximizing ...
> 
> *Attribution: Original Framing*
> *Source: zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Calibration-Engine|Calibration Engine]]*

> [!definition] **Calibration Event** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** An assessment or learning activity that produces an internally-generated encounter with the discrepancy between a learner's felt sense of knowing and their actual performance state. Distinguished from information delivery in that the signal cannot be attributed to external factors (test unfairness, unclear questions) because it is generated by the learner's own retrieval attempt, prediction, or explanation. **Boundary conditions:** not all assessment creates calibration events; assessment that allows passive recognition rather than active retrieval or prediction is not a cal...
> 
> *Source: formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Calibration-Event|Calibration Event]]*

> [!definition] **Categorical Syllogism** **[perm-note]**
> A deductive argument consisting of two premises and a conclusion, each of which is a categorical statement expressing an inclusion, exclusion, or partial overlap between classes. The medieval tradition identified 256 possible syllogistic forms, of which 24 are valid. The canonical example (*Barbara*) has the form "All M are P; All S are M; therefore All S are P." What it is NOT: a categorical syllogism is not any three-sentence argument — the premises must express class relationships and the conclusion must be entailed by the structure, not the content.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Categorical-Syllogism|Categorical Syllogism]]*

> [!definition] **Causal Attribution** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The explanatory accounts that individuals generate for their successes and failures, analyzed along the dimensions of locus (internal/external), stability (stable/unstable), and controllability (controllable/uncontrollable).
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The most consequential distinction for self-regulated learners is between *stable-internal-uncontrollable* attributions ("I failed to understand this because I'm not smart enough for this material") and *unstable-internal-controllable* attributions ("I failed to understand this section because I hadn't adequately review...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causal-Attribution|Causal Attribution]]*

> [!definition] **Causal Attribution in SRL** **[perm-note]**
> The explanatory account a learner generates to explain their performance outcomes. Attributions have consequences not just for how learners *feel* about their performance but for what they *do* next: specifically, whether they adjust strategies (controllable internal attributions) or give up / blame circumstances (uncontrollable or external attributions).
> 
> **Boundary:** Attribution is a *belief* about causes, not an accurate analysis of causes. Maladaptive attributions are false in the sense that they systematically misattribute causation — but they feel entirely correct to the learner maki...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causal-Attribution-in-SRL|Causal Attribution in SRL]]*

> [!definition] **Causal Induction** **[perm-note]**
> Inductive inference specifically aimed at identifying causal relationships: the inference that one event or condition reliably produces another. Research by Alison Gopnik and colleagues demonstrates that even infants engage in causal induction, updating causal beliefs in ways consistent with Bayesian prediction. Causal induction underlies scientific hypothesis formation, medical diagnosis, troubleshooting, and everyday understanding of how events produce consequences.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causal-Induction|Causal Induction]]*

> [!definition] **Causal Inference** **[perm-note]**
> A causal inference is an inference specifying causal antecedents or consequences of described events — the *why* and *how* underlying the text's narrative or explanatory structure. Causal inferences are generated with higher priority and greater automaticity than other elaborative inference types, consistent with Constructionist Theory's explanation assumption. They are the primary mechanism through which the causal dimension of the situation model (per the Event Indexing Model) is populated, and their quality is the strongest single predictor of subsequent comprehension performance.
> 
> **Res...
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causal-Inference|Causal Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Causal Schemata** **[perm-note]**
> **Causal schemata** (Kelley, 1972) are abstract knowledge structures — templates for causal reasoning — that are applied when complete covariation information is unavailable. Rather than performing full ANOVA-style analysis, people apply pre-formed schemas such as the **Multiple Sufficient Causes Schema** (if any one of several possible causes would be sufficient to produce the effect, the presence of one cause discounts inference to others) and the **Multiple Necessary Causes Schema** (if multiple causes are all required to produce an effect, the presence of one supports inference that oth...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causal-Schemata|Causal Schemata]]*

> [!definition] **Causal Spine** **[perm-note]**
> The primary causal chain running through a text or argument — the sequence of causally connected claims (X causes Y, which produces Z) that constitutes the structural backbone of the situation model. Other CATSM dimensions (agency, time, space, motivation) achieve coherence only when organized around a causal spine.
> 
> **Boundary:** The causal spine is not every causal claim in the text but the *primary* causal chain — the chain without which the text's central argument or explanation cannot be understood.
> 
> **Operational Indicator:** A reader has identified the causal spine when they can writ...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causal-Spine|Causal Spine]]*

> [!definition] **Causal-Attribution** **[perm-note]**
> Weiner's (1985) framework for understanding how outcomes are explained causally, across three dimensions: locus (internal/external), stability (stable/unstable), and controllability (controllable/uncontrollable). The most adaptive attribution pattern for learning failures: internal, unstable, controllable (strategy and effort).
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-applied-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causal-Attribution]]*

> [!definition] **Causality Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A stable individual-difference tendency to orient toward certain features of the motivational environment and to regulate behavior in characteristic ways. Three causality orientations are theorized: autonomy, control, and impersonal. Orientations are NOT mutually exclusive types — they are independent dimensions measured by separate GCOS subscales, and any individual has some degree of each. The term "causality" refers to the perceived locus of causality for behavior — whether behavior is experienced as self-caused (autonomous), other-caused (controlled), or beyond intentional causation (im...
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causality-Orientation|Causality Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Causality Orientations** *[needs-note]*
> Individual differences in the chronic tendency to interpret and engage with social environments in autonomy-consistent (autonomy orientation), pressure-compliance (control orientation), or helpless-amotivated (impersonal orientation) ways. Causality orientations moderate the relationship between relational conditions and relational motivation outcomes: high autonomy orientation individuals extract more autonomous motivation from relational contexts than objective conditions would predict; high control orientation individuals create controlled motivational experiences even in nominally suppo...
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causality-Orientations|Causality Orientations]]*

> [!definition] **Causality Orientations Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A mini-theory within SDT that describes individual differences in people's tendencies to orient toward particular types of motivational regulation. Not to be confused with [[Attribution-Theory]]. COT predicts that autonomy-oriented individuals will respond differently to controlling events than control-oriented individuals, and this moderates the functional significance mechanism in CET. An important implication: identical instructional interventions will produce different motivational outcomes for individuals with different causality orientations.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Causality-Orientations-Theory|Causality Orientations Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Central Executive** **[perm-note]**
> The attention-controlling component of the working memory system. Unlike the phonological loop and visuospatial sketchpad, the central executive is not a storage buffer but an attentional controller that supervises and coordinates the slave systems, switches between tasks, directs attention toward relevant information, suppresses irrelevant information, and integrates information from different sources. Baddeley has characterised it as a limited-capacity attentional system analogous to [[Norman-and-Shallice]]'s Supervisory Attentional System (SAS). The central executive is the most theoreti...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Central-Executive|Central Executive]]*

> [!definition] **The Central Executive** *[needs-note]*
> The **central executive** is the attentional-executive component of working memory responsible for coordinating the activities of the slave systems, directing attention to relevant sources of information, inhibiting irrelevant information or prepotent responses, switching between tasks or mental sets, and integrating information across working memory subsystems. Unlike the phonological loop and visuospatial sketchpad, the central executive is a control system rather than a storage system — it has no dedicated storage capacity but operates by managing the limited attentional resources of the...
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Central-Executive|The Central Executive]]*

> [!definition] **CET's Four Propositions** **[perm-note]**
> [**CET-Four-Propositions-Summary**:: (I) External events affect intrinsic motivation via PLOC and Perceived Competence; (II) Interpersonal context moderates the functional significance of specific events; (III) Controlling and amotivating events undermine intrinsic motivation; informational events can enhance it; (IV) Positive feedback enhances intrinsic motivation only when delivered informationally within autonomy-supportive contexts, not when delivered as evaluative pressure.]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[CET's-Four-Propositions|CET's Four Propositions]]*

> [!definition] **Chain-of-Thought Prompting** **[perm-note]**
> [**Chain-of-Thought-Prompting**:: A prompting methodology that elicits intermediate reasoning steps from language models by providing few-shot exemplars demonstrating explicit step-by-step problem decomposition, enabling the model to generate similar reasoning traces before producing final answers.]^verified
> 
> *Source: prompt-report-chain-of-thought-logic-2025122305* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Chain-of-Thought-Prompting|Chain-of-Thought Prompting]]*

> [!definition] **Challenge Avoidance** **[perm-note]**
> The motivational pattern in which a learner systematically selects tasks and situations that are not threatening to self-concept — typically easier tasks where success is highly likely. Characteristic of fixed-mindset orientation. Challenge avoidance is functionally rational within a fixed-mindset framework (protecting self-concept from diagnostic failure) but has significant developmental costs: easier tasks offer less learning, and chronic avoidance of challenge limits long-term skill development.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Challenge-Avoidance|Challenge Avoidance]]*

> [!definition] **Channel Congruence** **[perm-note]**
> The alignment of verbal, paraverbal (tonal), and nonverbal communicative signals around the same message. High channel congruence generates trust and clear interpretation; incongruence (conflicting signals across channels) triggers defensive interpretation, with nonverbal channels typically dominating in receiver interpretation. See [[Emotional-Intelligence]], [[Prosody]].
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Channel-Congruence|Channel Congruence]]*

> [!definition] **Channel Incongruence** **[perm-note]**
> When verbal and nonverbal signals carry contradictory messages — as when someone says "I'm fine" while their posture is closed, their voice flat, and their eye contact averted — the receiver is confronted with a paradoxical message. Research consistently shows that in such cases, listeners weight the nonverbal channels more heavily in their interpretation of the speaker's actual state. This is why skilled interpersonal communicators attend carefully to channel congruence: the degree to which all communicative channels align around the same message.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Channel-Incongruence|Channel Incongruence]]*

> [!definition] **Choice Function** **[perm-note]**
> The `choice()` function enables conditional rendering: `choice(condition, value_if_true, value_if_false)`
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-in-note-metadata-panel-dataview-inline-queries-2025120318* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Choice-Function|Choice Function]]*

> [!definition] **Chunk** **[perm-note]**
> A [[chunk|chunk]] is a cognitive unit representing a meaningful pattern of domain-specific information, stored in [[Long-Term-Memory|long-term memory]] and retrievable as a single item in [[Working-Memory|working memory]]. Chunks are characterized by (a) internal coherence — the elements within a chunk are bound by meaningful relationships rather than arbitrary association; (b) hierarchical organization — chunks can contain sub-chunks and can themselves be embedded within larger superordinate chunks; and (c) associative richness — each chunk is linked to other chunks, to procedural knowledg...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[chunk]]*

> [!definition] **Chunking** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive process of grouping individual items of information into larger, meaningful units (chunks) that can be processed as a single unit in working memory. Chunking reduces the effective number of items held in working memory by mapping information onto pre-existing long-term memory structures. An expert chess player perceives chess configurations as chunks; a novice perceives individual pieces. Chunking is the primary mechanism by which long-term knowledge expands effective working memory capacity. It is not a deliberate strategy so much as an automatic consequence of having the rel...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Chunking]]*

> [!definition] **Classical Cognitivism** **[perm-note]**
> The theoretical framework, dominant in cognitive science from the 1960s–1990s, holding that cognition consists in computational processes over internal, brain-bound representations. The mind is understood as an information-processing system with a principled boundary at the organism's skin or skull. External environments figure only as inputs and outputs, never as constituents of cognitive processes proper.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Classical-Cognitivism|Classical Cognitivism]]*

> [!definition] **Client-Server Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> MCP follows a <span style='color: #72FFF1;'>client-server architecture</span> where an <span style='color: #FFC700;'>MCP Host</span> (an AI application like [[Claude-Code]] or [[Claude-Desktop]]) establishes connections to one or more <span style='color: #FFC700;'>MCP Servers</span> through <span style='color: #FFC700;'>MCP Clients</span> (connection managers).
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-mcp-servers-2025122412* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Client-Server-Architecture|Client-Server Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Clinical Psychology** *[needs-note]*
> The scientific discipline and applied profession concerned with the assessment, understanding, prevention, and treatment of psychological distress, dysfunction, and disorder across the lifespan. Clinical psychology integrates scientific research (understanding mechanisms of psychopathology and therapeutic change) with clinical practice (applying that understanding to individuals and groups). It is distinguished from psychiatry by its non-medical training and historically non-pharmacological interventions; from counseling psychology by its traditional emphasis on more severe psychopathology;...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Clinical-Psychology|Clinical Psychology]]*

> [!definition] **Co-Constructed Criteria** **[perm-note]**
> Evaluative standards that are developed collaboratively by learners and educators, rather than unilaterally imposed. Co-construction serves two functions: it produces criteria that are better calibrated to learners' current understanding and language, and — critically — it shifts evaluative authority partially toward the learner, supporting the internalisation of standards necessary for self-assessment. Associated with greater learner engagement with feedback and more sophisticated self-monitoring.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Co-Constructed-Criteria|Co-Constructed Criteria]]*

> [!definition] **Co-Developmental Constraint** **[perm-note]**
> The co-developmental constraint is the structural feature of domain-specific metacognitive monitoring whereby monitoring accuracy cannot substantially surpass domain competence, because accurate assessment of performance quality requires a representation of what excellent performance looks like that can only be constructed through substantial domain experience. First articulated here as an original framing of the Dunning-Kruger data and related monitoring accuracy literature.
> 
> **See also:** [[Metacognitive-Calibration]], [[Dunning-Kruger-Effect]], [[Metacognitive-Monitoring]], [[Domain-Spec...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-development-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Co-Developmental-Constraint|Co-Developmental Constraint]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Architecture in Reading** *[needs-note]*
> The cognitive architecture of reading refers to the set of memory systems, processing capacities, and neural structures that enable and constrain text comprehension. In the context of academic reading, the most critical architectural elements are working memory (capacity and management), long-term memory (schema availability and retrieval), and the processing mechanisms that translate perceptual input into meaningful representations. Understanding this architecture is prerequisite to understanding comprehension failures and designing effective interventions.
> 
> **Boundary:** "Cognitive archit...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Architecture-in-Reading|Cognitive Architecture in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Bandwidth Liberation** **[perm-note]**
> An original analytical synthesis from this report. The principle that the educational project of fluency development is fundamentally about expanding the effective cognitive bandwidth available for comprehension by driving the resource cost of decoding toward zero — achieved through the automatization of orthographic schemas via high-volume practice. Unifies [[Cognitive-Load-Theory]], [[Dual-Process-Theory]], and [[Focal-Subsidiary-Attention-Distinction]] accounts of fluency under a single architectural principle.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Bandwidth-Liberation|Cognitive Bandwidth Liberation]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Bias** **[perm-note]**
> A systematic, predictable pattern of deviation from what would be expected under ideal-normative models of reasoning — models that assume full information processing, coherent probability assessment, and goal-directed rationality. Cognitive biases are not random errors; they are consistent distortions that arise from the architecture of human cognition. In political contexts, the most consequential biases involve the selective processing of information in ways that confirm existing beliefs and group identities. Importantly, cognitive biases are not signs of stupidity — they affect all reaso...
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Bias|Cognitive Bias]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Capture Buffer** **[perm-note]**
> In PKM design, a zero-friction, universally accessible input layer that records external cognitive content (ideas, references, quotes, observations) at the point of encounter with minimal processing requirements. The capture buffer decouples the timing of initial externalisation from the timing of full integration, allowing the practitioner to offload working memory contents immediately without the overhead of full processing. Functionally equivalent to the phonological loop's role in the multicomponent working memory model: a rapidly accessible temporary store that holds material pending d...
> 
> *Source: pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Capture-Buffer|Cognitive Capture Buffer]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Conflict** *[needs-note]*
> The state of disequilibrium produced when new experiences cannot be assimilated into existing schemas, creating tension that motivates schema accommodation (restructuring). In educational contexts, cognitive conflict is the deliberate instructional trigger for conceptual change when existing schemas are incorrect or insufficient. See also: [[Equilibration]], [[Assimilation-and-Accommodation]].
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Conflict|Cognitive Conflict]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Constructivism** **[perm-note]**
> **Cognitive constructivism** (Jean Piaget's framework) holds that children and learners construct logical and conceptual structures through active interaction with the physical and social environment. Construction is neither programmed by genes alone nor impressed by experience alone, but emerges from the dynamic interaction between organism and environment through the processes of [[Assimilation-and-Accommodation]], [[Equilibration]], and progressive reorganization of cognitive [[schemas]]. The focus is on the individual learner as a self-regulating, organism that constructs an increasingl...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Constructivism|Cognitive Constructivism]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Debt** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #27FF00;'>**Cognitive debt** is the compounding deficit in psychological and developmental well-being arising from the persistent enactment of cognitive schemas, [[heuristics]], and [[Cognitive-Biases]] that favor short-term, cognitively efficient responses over long-term, analytically optimal behaviors</span>. This debt manifests across two primary dimensions: an **internal dimension** (cognitive dissonance, reduced metacognitive awareness, diminished sense of agency) and an **external dimension** (suboptimal decisions, missed opportunities, relationship conflicts, care...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-compounding-negative-impact-of-unmitigated-cognitive-biases-202512150535* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Debt|Cognitive Debt]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Disequilibrium** **[perm-note]**
> The functional state that arises when an existing schema encounters experience it cannot adequately account for — when assimilation fails or produces internal contradictions — motivating accommodative restructuring. Cognitive disequilibrium is not simply confusion or frustration; it is the productive tension of recognizing that one's current understanding is inadequate while having sufficient competence to recognize the inadequacy.
> 
> **See also:** [[Cognitive-Disequilibrium]], [[Equilibration]], [[Assimilation-and-Accommodation]], [[Conceptual-Change]]
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Disequilibrium|Cognitive Disequilibrium]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Evaluation Theory** **[perm-note]**
> SDT's sub-theory addressing the effects of external events (rewards, feedback, deadlines, surveillance) on intrinsic motivation. CET proposes that any external event carries both an informational aspect (conveying something about competence) and a controlling aspect (conveying something about who is determining behavior). The relative salience of these two aspects determines whether the external event enhances (informational dominant) or undermines (controlling dominant) intrinsic motivation.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Evaluation-Theory|Cognitive Evaluation Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Evaluation Theory — CET** *[needs-note]*
> Cognitive Evaluation Theory is the mini-theory within [[Self-Determination-Theory]] that specifies the conditions under which external events — rewards, feedback, surveillance, deadlines, competitive structures — affect intrinsic motivation. CET proposes that external events have three aspects of functional significance: informational (competence-relevant, autonomy-preserving), controlling (autonomy-undermining, PLOC-shifting), and amotivating (competence-disconfirming). Informational events enhance intrinsic motivation; controlling events undermine it; amotivating events collapse it. The t...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Evaluation-Theory-—-CET|Cognitive Evaluation Theory — CET]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Load** **[perm-note]**
> [**Cognitive-Load**:: <span style='color: #27FF00;'>the total mental effort being deployed in working memory during information processing, measured across three distinct dimensions: intrinsic load (inherent task complexity), extraneous load (imposed by poor design), and germane load (productive effort dedicated to schema construction and automation)</span>.]
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-examination-of-cognitive-load-theory-and-the-attentional-bottleneck-202512170123* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Load|Cognitive Load]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Load in Communication** **[perm-note]**
> The total processing demand placed on a receiver's working memory during a communicative exchange — comprising intrinsic load (inherent complexity of content), extraneous load (how the message is structured and delivered), and germane load (effort devoted to integrating new information with existing schemas). Communication design that minimizes extraneous load (through structure, signposting, and calibration to receiver knowledge) improves comprehension and retention.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-04* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Load-in-Communication|Cognitive Load in Communication]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Load Theory** **[perm-note]**
> An instructional design theory asserting that human learning is constrained by the limited capacity of [[Working-Memory]], and that effective instruction must manage the cognitive load imposed on working memory during learning. The theory distinguishes three types of load — intrinsic, extraneous, and germane — which are theorized to be additive within working memory's fixed capacity. The theory has generated numerous specific instructional prescriptions (worked example effect, split-attention effect, redundancy effect, guidance fading principle) with varying degrees of empirical support. CL...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Load-Theory|Cognitive Load Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Niche** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive niche is the culturally and individually constructed environment of tools, practices, representations, and social structures that extended human minds both inhabit and produce. The cognitive niche is not merely instrumental (a collection of useful tools) but constitutive: it creates the conditions for forms of cognition that would be impossible without it. Language, writing, mathematics, and institutionalized knowledge represent layers of cognitive niche construction that, once internalized by individual minds, scaffold forms of thinking beyond what unaided biological cognitio...
> 
> *Source: schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Niche|Cognitive Niche]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Offloading** **[perm-note]**
> The use of the environment to reduce the cognitive demands placed on internal biological cognitive systems. Offloading encompasses strategies ranging from writing things down to arranging physical objects spatially to using digital reminders. Importantly, offloading is not the same as cognitive extension in Clark and Chalmers's sense: offloading is a strategy for reducing cognitive load, while extension is a claim about the actual constitution of the cognitive system. The distinction is subtle but theoretically important: a person can offload extensively without any of the offloaded process...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Offloading|Cognitive Offloading]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Restructuring** *[needs-note]*
> A core technique within cognitive-behavioral approaches in which maladaptive automatic thoughts, dysfunctional assumptions, and core schemas are systematically identified, examined using Socratic questioning, evaluated against available evidence, and replaced or modified through the development of more adaptive alternative beliefs. Cognitive restructuring treats cognitions as hypotheses subject to empirical testing rather than as facts, and involves both cognitive interventions (examining evidence, generating alternatives) and behavioral experiments (testing predictions through action). Not...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Restructuring|Cognitive Restructuring]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Scaffolding** **[perm-note]**
> Environmental, social, or material structures that support cognitive performance by organising, simplifying, and augmenting the cognitive work an individual must perform. Scaffolding can be temporary (faded as competence develops) or permanent (constituting an ongoing cognitive extension). The concept derives from Vygotsky's Zone of Proximal Development and has been developed in instructional design, where it describes structured support for learners. Extended Mind Theory provides a theoretical account of how scaffolding can transition from external support to genuine cognitive extension.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Scaffolding|Cognitive Scaffolding]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Slack** **[perm-note]**
> The available working memory capacity remaining after a reader has allocated resources to text comprehension and decoding — the residual cognitive budget from which vocabulary inference draws during reading. Cognitive slack is zero or negative when a text demands all available working memory for comprehension; it is positive when reading is automatic and fluent enough that capacity remains.
> 
> **Boundary:** "Cognitive slack" is a theoretical construct introduced in this report to describe the mechanistic link between the coverage threshold and vocabulary acquisition rates. It is not a standar...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Slack|Cognitive Slack]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Surface Area** **[perm-note]**
> The original synthesis introduced in Phase VI: the total cognitive interface between what the writer currently believes and what the writing task demands of those beliefs. Maximised by retrieval-writing for naive audiences in causal/inferential formats. The measure by which different writing tasks can be ranked for their learning-transformative potential.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Surface-Area|Cognitive Surface Area]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning** **[perm-note]**
> Mayer's theoretical framework for understanding how people learn from words and pictures together. CTML predicts that presenting information in both verbal and visual forms — when the formats are designed to minimize extraneous load and support generative processing — produces superior learning to either format alone. The theory generates twelve instructional principles tested through controlled experiments, all derived from the core premise that well-designed multimedia supports the cognitive processes of selection, organization, and integration through which meaning is actively constructed.
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Theory-of-Multimedia-Learning|Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive Transparency Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The tacit assumption that learners have sufficiently accurate introspective access to their own cognitive states to support effective regulation. Not equivalent to consciousness — the assumption is specifically that *conscious* access to cognitive states (monitoring of comprehension, awareness of cognitive effort, assessment of understanding) accurately represents the underlying cognitive states being monitored. Post-reconstruction: does not survive in strong form; requires supplementation with external calibration mechanisms.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The philosoph...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Transparency-Assumption|Cognitive Transparency Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Cognitive-Psychological Conceptualization** **[perm-note]**
> **[CT-Cognitive-Process-Model**:: From cognitive science perspective, critical thinking comprises specific, measurable cognitive operations including semantic analysis (extracting meaning from text/speech), logical evaluation (assessing argument validity), evidence appraisal (judging claim support), inference generation (drawing conclusions), and synthesis (integrating multiple perspectives) - all executed within working memory constraints and regulated by executive control and metacognitive monitoring.]**
> 
> This operationalization transforms CT from abstract virtue into analyzable phenomeno...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-foundational-critical-thinking* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cognitive-Psychological-Conceptualization|Cognitive-Psychological Conceptualization]]*

> [!definition] **Common Factors Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The theoretical framework proposing that the therapeutic gains observed across diverse forms of psychotherapy are attributable primarily to elements common to all credible therapies — including a healing context, a rationale or explanatory framework offered by a trained professional, and therapeutic procedures experienced as logical and credible — rather than to the specific techniques that distinguish individual therapy approaches. Associated primarily with Jerome Frank's concept of "nonspecific factors" and developed into the Contextual Model by Bruce Wampold. Common factors theorists do ...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Common-Factors-Theory|Common Factors Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Comparative Advance Organizer** **[perm-note]**
> An advance organizer used when the learner *does* have relevant prior knowledge — but where that knowledge needs to be made explicitly accessible and its relationship to the new material needs to be articulated. A comparative organizer does not provide new concepts; it draws explicit connections between the learner's existing schemas and the new material, highlighting both similarities (to exploit transfer) and critical differences (to prevent confusion). Example: before teaching electrical circuits to students who know about water flow systems, a comparative organizer would explicitly map ...
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Comparative-Advance-Organizer|Comparative Advance Organizer]]*

> [!definition] **Comparative Organizer** **[perm-note]**
> A type of advance organizer used when learners have relevant prior knowledge. Draws explicit parallels between existing knowledge structures and new material, facilitating transfer while clearly delineating the boundaries of the comparison. Compare: [[Expository-Organizer]].
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Comparative-Organizer|Comparative Organizer]]*

> [!definition] **Compensation Trap** **[perm-note]**
> A pattern in which a reader with inadequate decoding automaticity compensates by slowing down to maintain accuracy, producing low error rates on oral reading assessments while sustaining the working memory bottleneck and its comprehension consequences. The compensation trap is educationally dangerous because it renders the student invisible to accuracy-based screening while the Matthew Effect runs in the negative direction.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Compensation-Trap|Compensation Trap]]*

> [!definition] **Compensatory Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> The alternative to the conjunctive thesis: that the three needs are relatively independent contributors to well-being, each adding to the aggregate when satisfied, with high satisfaction of one or two capable of partially compensating for deficits in another.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Compensatory-Hypothesis|Compensatory Hypothesis]]*

> [!definition] **Compensatory Striving** **[perm-note]**
> **[[Compensatory-Striving]]** — The developmental and motivational process by which individuals who experience chronic frustration of basic psychological needs turn to extrinsic goal pursuit as a substitute source of self-worth and satisfaction. Because extrinsic attainment cannot directly satisfy the frustrated needs, compensatory striving tends to be self-perpetuating — each round of attainment provides diminishing returns, prompting further intensification of extrinsic pursuit. See Sections 3, 5.
> **See also:** [[Conditional-Regard]], [[Need-Frustration]], [[Contingent-Self-Worth]]
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Compensatory-Striving|Compensatory Striving]]*

> [!definition] **Competence** **[perm-note]**
> The need to experience oneself as effective in interaction with the environment — to feel capable, to master challenges, and to bring about desired outcomes. Competence in SDT is not an objective measure of ability but a psychological experience of effectance. This experience is sustained when challenges are optimally calibrated (sufficiently challenging to require effort, but achievable with effort), when feedback is informational and positive, and when progress is visible. Environments that are either too easy (no growth) or too difficult (repeated failure without scaffolding) undermine t...
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Competence]]*

> [!definition] **Competence Need** **[perm-note]**
> The need for [[Competence]] is the need to experience oneself as *effectively producing desired outcomes* in interaction with one's environment — to feel efficacious in the activities one undertakes. This is related to but distinct from [[Self-Efficacy]] as defined by Bandura: self-efficacy is domain-specific confidence about future performance capability, while the competence need refers to the ongoing experiential state of feeling capable and effective. The competence need is satisfied through *optimal challenge* — tasks that stretch current capacity without overwhelming it — and through ...
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Competence-Need|Competence Need]]*

> [!definition] **Complete Agent File Format** **[perm-note]**
> ```markdown
> ---
> name: agent-name
> description: When to invoke this agent (CRITICAL for auto-routing)
> tools: tool1, tool2, tool3  # Optional - inherits all if omitted
> model: sonnet|opus|haiku|inherit  # Optional - defaults to current
> permissionMode: default  # Optional
> skills: skill1, skill2  # Optional - auto-load specific skills
> ---
> 
> # System Prompt Content (Markdown)
> 
> You are an expert [domain specialist].
> 
> ## Role
> [Define specific responsibility]
> 
> ## When Invoked
> [Describe trigger conditions]
> 
> ## Workflow
> 1. [Step one]
> 2. [Step two]
> 3. [Return results in specific format]
> 
> ## Key Practices...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Complete-Agent-File-Format|Complete Agent File Format]]*

> [!definition] **Complete Project Structure** **[perm-note]**
> ```
> project-root/
> ├── .claude/
> │   ├── agents/                     # Project-specific agents
> │   │   ├── domain-expert.md       # Understands business logic
> │   │   ├── integration-tester.md  # Tests external API integrations
> │   │   └── architect.md           # System design specialist
> │   ├── commands/                   # Custom slash commands  
> │   │   ├── /review.md             # Comprehensive code review workflow
> │   │   ├── /deploy.md             # Deployment checklist execution
> │   │   └── /research.md           # Multi-agent research coordination
> │   ├── scripts/                    ...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Complete-Project-Structure|Complete Project Structure]]*

> [!definition] **Complex Span Tasks** **[perm-note]**
> **Complex Span Tasks** (Daneman & Carpenter, 1980): Working memory capacity measures that interleave processing demands (verifying equations, judging sentence acceptability, counting items) with storage demands (remembering a set of items for later recall). Complex span scores predict higher cognitive abilities (reading comprehension, reasoning, fluid intelligence) substantially more powerfully than simple span tasks (digit span) because they require central executive engagement — the controlled maintenance of information against concurrent demand.
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Complex-Span-Tasks|Complex Span Tasks]]*

> [!definition] **Comprehension** **[perm-note]**
> In this analysis, comprehension refers specifically to the construction of a [[Situation-Model]]: a mental representation of the situation described by the text, including not only what was explicitly stated but the inferences, causal relationships, and world-knowledge integrations needed to make the text coherent. Surface comprehension (remembering specific sentences) is a weaker standard; situation model construction is the goal of skilled reading.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Comprehension]]*

> [!definition] **Comprehension Criterion** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** An operationally specific statement, set in the Forethought Phase, of what "understanding" means for a given reading session — a standard that requires *cognitive production* (recall, explanation, application, connection-making) to satisfy, not merely recognition. **Boundary Conditions:** Must be verifiable through a production task, not through subjective sense of fluency. **Report-Specific Significance:** The comprehension criterion is the structural counter to the fluency illusion — it is the mechanism through which self-evaluation in the Reflection Phase becomes epistemi...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)* | *Domain: self-regulated-learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[Comprehension-Criterion|Comprehension Criterion]]*

> [!definition] **Comprehension Gateway Mechanism** **[perm-note]**
> The mechanism by which elaborative interrogation produces its comprehension and retention benefits: a Register 2 "why" question imposes a demand structure that textbase-level retrieval cannot satisfy, structurally forcing the reader into situation model construction territory.
> 
> **Boundary:** The gateway mechanism only operates for Register 2 questions. Register 1 questions (answerable from the text) do not function as gateways — they allow textbase responses and therefore do not force situation model construction.
> 
> **Epistemic Status:** Original synthesis — well-motivated by converging evid...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Comprehension-Gateway-Mechanism|Comprehension Gateway Mechanism]]*

> [!definition] **Comprehension Monitoring** **[perm-note]**
> The process of evaluating one's own text comprehension during reading — detecting failures of understanding, inconsistencies between text and background knowledge, and gaps in situational coherence. Comprehension monitoring is a specific application of [[Metacognitive-Monitoring]] to the reading domain. It is distinguished from comprehension *testing* (external assessment of comprehension) by its real-time, self-directed character.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Comprehension-Monitoring|Comprehension Monitoring]]*

> [!definition] **Comprehension Monitoring Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The vocabulary used during in-progress reading to characterize one's current comprehension state. The granularity and structural affordances of this vocabulary determine the precision of metacognitive monitoring available to the reader. Coarse-grained vocabulary ("I get it" / "I don't get it") provides binary monitoring access. Fine-grained vocabulary ("I understand the claim structure but haven't yet mapped the evidence," "I'm missing the conceptual bridge between section two and three," "I've grasped the surface argument but the deeper logic is unclear") provides graduated...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Comprehension-Monitoring-Vocabulary|Comprehension Monitoring Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Concepts** **[perm-note]**
> **Concepts** are the organizing ideas, theories, principles, definitions, models, or axioms that structure thinking. They provide the abstract frameworks through which we understand and categorize experience. Concepts answer: *What key ideas am I using to organize my thinking? What theories shape my understanding?* All reasoning necessarily employs concepts—we cannot think without conceptual frameworks any more than we can see without eyes.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-fundamental-structures-of-reasoning-202512050320* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Concepts]]*

> [!definition] **Conceptual Change** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: Deep learning that involves not accretion to existing conceptual frameworks but the restructuring or replacement of those frameworks with qualitatively different explanatory systems. Requires: dissatisfaction with current conception, intelligibility of the new conception, plausibility of the new conception, and fruitfulness of the new conception. NOT equivalent to Rumelhart's "restructuring" — it is more specific about the conditions and mechanism.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The reconstructive resource that provides what schema theory's own mechanism (accommodation) f...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Conceptual-Change|Conceptual Change]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Knowledge about when, why, and under what conditions specific learning strategies are effective. Provides the causal vocabulary necessary for strategy-level attribution during the Self-Reflection Phase. Without conditional knowledge, learners are restricted to coarser attribution dimensions (ability, effort) that carry worse motivational consequences than strategy attributions.
> 
> *Source: zimmerman-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[conditional-metacognitive-knowledge|Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional Positive Regard** **[perm-note]**
> The relational pattern in which warmth, approval, and belonging are made contingent on the partner's compliance with desired behavior or self-presentation. CPR is the quintessential autonomy-thwarting mechanism in RMT; its long-term effect is the development of contingent self-esteem — a form of self-regard that fluctuates with performance on internalized conditions of worth, producing instability, anxiety, and self-monitoring at the expense of autonomous self-determination.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Conditional-Positive-Regard|Conditional Positive Regard]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional Prescription Problem** **[perm-note]**
> The structural challenge (identified in Phase VI) faced by any prescriptive framework that generates recommendations for supporting a developing system: prescriptions valid for early developmental states become counterproductive in later states, as the support needed for early-stage performance constrains late-stage expert performance. The expertise reversal effect is the CLT-specific instantiation of this more general problem.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Conditional-Prescription-Problem|Conditional Prescription Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional Regard** **[perm-note]**
> Teacher or parent warmth that is contingent on student performance or compliance — love and approval are available when the student succeeds but withheld when the student fails or resists. A documented form of relatedness thwarting that produces introjected regulation (students internalize the contingency — "I am valuable when I perform") rather than identified or integrated regulation. Associated with high anxiety, fragile self-esteem, and vulnerability to collapse of motivation under failure conditions.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Conditional-Regard|Conditional Regard]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional-Positive-Regard** **[perm-note]**
> The relational pattern in which approval, warmth, and positive regard are made contingent on meeting performance standards. Research within SDT demonstrates that conditional positive regard is a primary driver of introjected (rather than identified or integrated) regulation, because it communicates that the person's worth is performance-contingent and thus creates the relational threat that makes releasing the introjection feel existentially dangerous.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Conditional-Positive-Regard]]*

> [!definition] **Confirmation Bias** **[perm-note]**
> The systematic tendency to seek, notice, weight, and remember information that confirms existing beliefs, while neglecting, dismissing, or underweighting disconfirming information. Confirmation bias is the primary psychological mechanism by which fallibilism is violated in practice — not through explicit philosophical commitment to infallibilism but through motivated asymmetric processing of evidence. First formally identified in Peter Wason's selection task experiments (1960s).
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Confirmation-Bias|Confirmation Bias]]*

> [!definition] **Conflict Monitoring** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive mechanism by which inconsistencies between System 1 outputs and more deliberate reasoning are detected, generating a signal that can (but does not always) activate System 2 correction. The neural substrate of what Dewey called felt difficulty and what Socrates produced through elenchus.
> 
> *Source: report-01-the-inquiry-engine* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Conflict-Monitoring|Conflict Monitoring]]*

> [!definition] **Conjunctive System Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> The proposal that the three basic psychological needs are jointly necessary for full psychological well-being and optimal functioning — meaning all three must be adequately satisfied simultaneously, and persistent deprivation of any one creates well-being costs not offset by amplified satisfaction of the others.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Conjunctive-System-Architecture|Conjunctive System Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> The set of processes by which newly formed, initially fragile memory representations are stabilized over time. Consolidation occurs at two levels: *synaptic consolidation* (cellular/molecular stabilization of individual synaptic changes, occurring over hours and requiring protein synthesis) and *systems consolidation* (gradual transfer of memory representation from hippocampus-dependent to neocortically distributed storage, occurring over weeks to years). Consolidation can be disrupted by trauma, sleep deprivation, or pharmacological intervention during the critical post-encoding window.
> 
> *...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Consolidation]]*

> [!definition] **Constitutive Vocabulary Function** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The property of vocabulary whereby the act of formulating an experience in specific words *partially creates* the psychological reality of that experience, rather than merely describing a pre-formed reality. Distinguished from *expressive vocabulary function* (vocabulary as symptom of prior state) and from *performative speech acts* (vocabulary that creates social facts). In the motivational inner speech context, constitutive vocabulary means that the words chosen to characterize a comprehension state, attribute a failure, or frame a goal are among the causes — not merely th...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Constitutive-Vocabulary-Function|Constitutive Vocabulary Function]]*

> [!definition] **Construction Problem** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The unresolved difficulty that schema theory cannot explain the origin of schemas without presupposing schemas. If all learning is mediated by schemas (through assimilation or accommodation), the formation of the first schema is inexplicable within schema theory's own resources. NOT merely an abstract philosophical puzzle; it reveals the limits of schema theory's foundational claim. After this re-examination, dissolved by the attractor account (schemas are emergent from statistical learning, not foundational primitives).
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Identified as the de...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Construction-Problem|Construction Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Construction-Activation Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The categorical difference between (1) schema activation — fast, automatic, top-down retrieval and application of an existing schema guided by partial cues — and (2) schema construction — slow, effortful, bottom-up building of a new schema through analogy and restructuring. The asymmetry has both mechanistic and resource dimensions: different cognitive processes are at work, requiring different cognitive resources and different time scales.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** The asymmetry applies to cognitive schemas; related asymmetries have been identified in skill acquisition (bet...
> 
> *Attribution: original synthesis*
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Construction-Activation-Asymmetry|Construction-Activation Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Construction-Integration** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A computational model of text comprehension proposing two cyclically alternating phases: construction (building an initial network of propositions from text plus associated prior knowledge activations) and integration (refining the network through spreading activation to produce a coherent representation). The situation model emerges from successful integration cycles.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Provides the mechanistic account of why the received view fails: poor situation models result from inadequate integration cycles, not inadequate construction — and integratio...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Construction-Integration]]*

> [!definition] **Construction-Integration Model** **[perm-note]**
> A two-phase computational model of discourse comprehension: the Construction phase activates relevant concepts and relationships from text and long-term memory, forming an initial (noisy, over-inclusive) network; the Integration phase settles this network through constraint satisfaction, producing a coherent mental representation. The model produces all three levels of representation: surface code, textbase, and situation model.
> 
> **Boundary:** The CI model is a computational model, not a phenomenological description. It does not imply that readers are conscious of construction and integrati...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[Construction-Integration-Model|Construction-Integration Model]]*

> [!definition] **Constructionist Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A theory of inference generation specifying that causal antecedent inferences (explaining why events occurred) and goal-motive inferences are generated automatically during comprehension, while predictive and elaborative inferences are not. Based on three assumptions: reader goal (inference generation is goal-modulated), coherence (readers default to seeking coherent representations), and explanation (readers automatically seek causal explanations).
> 
> **See also:** [[Situation-Model]], [[Self-Explanation-Effect]], [[Metacognitive-Scaffolding]]
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Constructionist-Theory|Constructionist Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Constructive Threshold** **[perm-note]**
> The point in a reading episode at which inference generation shifts from elaborative (enriching) to evaluative (assessing) operations. Proposed in this analysis as a key gatekeeper for critical analysis development. Phonological load is identified as a primary factor determining whether a reader crosses this threshold.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Constructive-Threshold|Constructive Threshold]]*

> [!definition] **Constructivism** **[perm-note]**
> Constructivism, as an epistemological position, holds that knowledge is not discovered or received but actively constructed by the knower through interaction with the environment. Cognitive structures — schemas, concepts, frameworks — are not copies of reality but are built by the learner and continuously revised in response to experience. The key distinction is between learning as acquisition (the receipt of pre-formed knowledge packages) and learning as construction (the active building and rebuilding of meaning structures).
> 
> **Boundary:** Constructivism does not deny an external reality,...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Constructivism]]*

> [!definition] **Constructivism Defined** **[perm-note]**
> **Constructivism** is an epistemological and psychological framework holding that knowledge is not a fixed, external reality to be transmitted from teacher to learner, but an active construction of the knowing subject built through experience, prior knowledge, and — in social variants — collaborative interaction. The learner is not a passive vessel but an active meaning-maker who interprets, organizes, and transforms incoming information through existing cognitive structures. The claim is simultaneously epistemological (concerning the nature of knowledge) and psychological (concerning the m...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[Constructivism-Defined|Constructivism Defined]]*

> [!definition] **Constructivist Pedagogy** **[perm-note]**
> **Constructivist pedagogy** encompasses the family of instructional approaches, methods, and design principles derived from or consistent with constructivist learning theory. Common characteristics include: activating and connecting to prior knowledge; presenting learning tasks that create productive cognitive challenge (not passive reception); using authentic, complex, context-embedded problems rather than decontextualized exercises; supporting collaborative knowledge construction; requiring learners to make thinking visible through explanation, representation, and argument; using formativ...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[Constructivist-Pedagogy|Constructivist Pedagogy]]*

> [!definition] **Content-by-Motive Interaction** **[perm-note]**
> **Content-by-Motive Interaction** — A theoretical refinement within GCT recognizing that the well-being consequences of goal pursuit depend jointly on the *content* of the goal (intrinsic vs. extrinsic) and the *regulatory motive* underlying its pursuit (autonomous vs. controlled). A goal with extrinsic content pursued for autonomous reasons may have different well-being consequences than the same goal pursued for controlled reasons. See Section 7.
> **See also:** [[Autonomous-Motivation]], [[Controlled-Motivation]], [[Regulatory-Style]]
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Content-by-Motive-Interaction|Content-by-Motive Interaction]]*

> [!definition] **Context Collapse** **[perm-note]**
> The dissolution of audience specificity in digital communication, where messages intended for targeted audiences reach heterogeneous, unanticipated audiences simultaneously. A source of systematic communication failure in social media and other digital communication platforms.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Context-Collapse|Context Collapse]]*

> [!definition] **Context Collapse in Digital Communication** **[perm-note]**
> *Context collapse* — a concept developed by danah boyd in social media research — describes the situation in digital communication where messages intended for specific audiences are instead visible to radically heterogeneous, unanticipated audiences simultaneously. The professional posting their political views on LinkedIn, the person whose private message is screenshotted and shared publicly, the speaker addressing one audience whose words are distributed to another: all experience context collapse. Managing context collapse requires either highly general communication (accessible and inno...
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Context-Collapse-in-Digital-Communication|Context Collapse in Digital Communication]]*

> [!definition] **Context Inference** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive process by which a reader uses surrounding textual information — syntactic cues, semantic associations, thematic expectations, world knowledge — to infer the approximate meaning of an unfamiliar word. The quality of a context for inference depends on: (1) how much relevant meaning-relevant information the surrounding text provides; (2) the distance between the target word and its inferential supports; (3) whether the meaning clues are explicit or require domain-specific background knowledge to interpret.
> 
> **Boundary:** Context inference is probabilistic and error-prone. Studie...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Context-Inference|Context Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Context of Discovery vs. Context of Justification** **[perm-note]**
> A foundational distinction in [[philosophy-of-science|philosophy of science]], associated with [[Hans-Reichenbach]] (1938), between the *context of discovery* (the psychological and heuristic process by which hypotheses are generated) and the *context of justification* (the logical process by which hypotheses are tested and confirmed or disconfirmed). Peirce's abduction belongs primarily to the context of discovery, though [[Inference-to-the-Best-Explanation]] attempts to give it a role in the context of justification as well. The distinction matters pedagogically: abductive reasoning instr...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Context-of-Discovery-vs.-Context-of-Justification|Context of Discovery vs. Context of Justification]]*

> [!definition] **Context Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Regulatory activities directed at managing features of the task and learning environment, including structuring the physical environment for study, managing social resources strategically, selecting and modifying tasks, and seeking or avoiding specific types of help. Context is treated as a first-class regulatory domain rather than a fixed background condition.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The most theoretically underspecified and empirically least-supported area in Pintrich's framework, but potentially the most consequential for learners whose learning failures are pr...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Context-Regulation|Context Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Context Window** **[perm-note]**
