> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Hierarchical-Goal-Architecture|Hierarchical Goal Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **The Eight Elements of Thought** **[perm-note]**
> 1. **[[Purpose-in-Reasoning|Purpose]]** — Every act of reasoning has a goal, objective, or function. Reasoning is always directed.
> 2. **[[Question-at-Issue|Question at Issue]]** — Every act of reasoning attempts to address a question, problem, or issue.
> 3. **[[Information]]** — Every act of reasoning uses data, facts, observations, or experiences as its evidential base.
> 4. **[[Interpretation-and-Inference|Interpretation and Inference]]** — Every act of reasoning involves drawing conclusions, making interpretations, and giving meaning to data.
> 5. **[[Concepts]]** — Every act of reasoning emp...
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Eight-Elements-of-Thought|The Eight Elements of Thought]]*

> [!definition] **Tight-Loop Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The implicit architectural commitment in the [[Zimmerman-SRL-Model]] that the three phases form a *functionally integrated* feedback loop in which information flows reliably from Performance Phase monitoring through Self-Reflection Phase evaluation to Forethought Phase modification. The assumption is architectural (embedded in the circular representation of the model) rather than propositional (stated as a testable claim). The dialectical process shows this assumption does not survive scrutiny in its strong form; a conditional version — that tight coupling is *possible* give...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Tight-Loop-Assumption|Tight-Loop Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Illusion of Explanatory Depth** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive phenomenon documented by Rozenblit and Keil (2002): people systematically overestimate their understanding of causal/mechanical systems before attempting to explain them. When asked to explain in detail, the overestimation collapses. The illusion arises because familiarity with the *function* of a system (what it does) is mistaken for understanding of its *mechanism* (how and why it does it). The phenomenon demonstrates that the *feeling* of understanding is an unreliable guide to its presence.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Illusion-of-Explanatory-Depth|Illusion of Explanatory Depth]]*

> [!definition] **Illusion of Knowing / Illusion of Comprehension** **[perm-note]**
> The illusion of knowing is a metacognitive failure mode in which a learner's subjective feeling of understanding a concept or text is not matched by their actual ability to use, apply, transfer, or accurately reproduce that knowledge when tested. The illusion arises from monitoring systems that use *processing fluency* (ease and speed of recognition and processing) as a proxy for *understanding depth* — a proxy that is accurate when familiar material is actually well-understood but is systematically misleading when familiarity reflects prior exposure rather than deep encoding. The illusion ...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Illusion-of-Knowing-Illusion-of-Comprehension|Illusion of Knowing / Illusion of Comprehension]]*

> [!definition] **Impersonal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> The tendency to orient toward the environment as if one's behavior were beyond intentional control — as if outcomes are determined by forces (internal or external) that cannot be managed. Individuals high in impersonal orientation characteristically experience themselves as incapable of producing desired outcomes, attribute performance to luck, fate, or overwhelming circumstances, and often disengage from effortful pursuit. Impersonal orientation is theoretically associated with [[Amotivation]] in OIT's motivational continuum and with [[Learned-Helplessness]] in Seligman's framework. Unlike...
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Impersonal-Orientation|Impersonal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Implementation Intention** **[perm-note]**
> **Implementation Intention** (Gollwitzer, 1999): A planning strategy that specifies the situation-behavior link for goal enactment in "if-then" format: "If [critical situational cue], then [goal-directed response]." The mechanism is prospective memory automatization: by pre-linking the behavioral response to a specific situational cue at encoding, the detection of that cue at retrieval automatically activates the intended behavior without requiring conscious deliberation. This bypasses the effortful access problem that causes non-implementation of mere goal intentions.
> 
> Boundary conditions:...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Implementation-Intention|Implementation Intention]]*

> [!definition] **Implications and Consequences** **[perm-note]**
> **Implications** are what logically follows from your reasoning—the conclusions that must be accepted if you accept your premises and reasoning. **Consequences** are the likely results or effects that would occur if your conclusions were acted upon. Together they answer: *If I accept this reasoning, what else follows? What would happen if I acted on these conclusions?* Tracing implications and consequences projects reasoning forward to examine what it commits you to.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-fundamental-structures-of-reasoning-202512050320* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Implications-and-Consequences|Implications and Consequences]]*

> [!definition] **Implicit Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> A lay person's intuitive, largely non-conscious theory about the nature of intelligence and ability. Unlike explicit, carefully reasoned positions, implicit theories are often tacit — operating in the background of cognition without being consciously articulated. They are called "implicit" not because they are unconscious in the Freudian sense but because people rarely surface them to deliberate examination. Research shows these theories powerfully predict behavior under conditions of challenge, setback, and evaluation.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Implicit-Theory-of-Intelligence|Implicit Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Incidental Acquisition from Context** **[perm-note]**
> The accrual of lexical knowledge as an unintended byproduct of reading for comprehension. Distinguished from *intentional learning* by the learner's primary goal (comprehension, not word learning) and from *rote memorisation* by the naturalness of the acquisition context.
> 
> **Boundary:** Incidental does not mean passive in the sense of zero attention. Some attention to the word form is necessary; "incidental" refers to the *goal structure*, not the attentional allocation.
> 
> **See also:** [[Incidental-Learning]], [[Metacognition-in-Learning]], [[Levels-of-Processing-Theory]]
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incidental-Acquisition-from-Context|Incidental Acquisition from Context]]*

> [!definition] **Incidental Learning** **[perm-note]**
> Learning that occurs without explicit intent to memorize, typically studied using cover stories that direct participants' attention away from any awareness that a memory test will follow. LOP research demonstrated that incidental semantic learners often remember as much as intentional learners, supporting the claim that processing depth, not intent, is the primary determinant of retention.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incidental-Learning|Incidental Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Incidental Vocabulary Acquisition** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which word meanings are acquired as a by-product of comprehension-focused reading or listening, without deliberate intention to learn the words encountered. Distinguished from *intentional vocabulary learning*, which involves explicit study (word cards, memorization, dictionary use) directed at acquiring specific words. The received view holds that incidental acquisition from reading is the dominant vocabulary learning mechanism for mature readers; the dialectical challenge concerns the efficiency and scope of this mechanism.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The entire deba...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incidental-Vocabulary-Acquisition|Incidental Vocabulary Acquisition]]*

> [!definition] **The Incremental Knowledge Model** **[perm-note]**
> A model of vocabulary knowledge according to which knowing a word is not binary (known or unknown) but involves gradual acquisition across multiple independent dimensions: (1) spoken/written form, (2) word parts and morphology, (3) syntagmatic relationships (collocations, typical sentence positions), (4) paradigmatic relationships (synonyms, antonyms, hyponyms), (5) semantic range and polysemy, (6) register and contextual appropriateness, (7) frequency and availability for production. Nation (2001) identifies nine aspects of word knowledge across three dimensions of form, meaning, and use, ...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Incremental-Knowledge-Model|The Incremental Knowledge Model]]*

> [!definition] **Incremental Knowledge Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A model of vocabulary knowledge according to which knowing a word involves gradual acquisition across multiple independent dimensions: spoken/written form, word parts, syntagmatic relationships (collocations), paradigmatic relationships (synonyms/antonyms), semantic range and polysemy, register and contextual appropriateness, and availability for production. Each dimension can be known receptively (recognition in context) or productively (availability for use). Word knowledge is not binary (known/unknown) but a probability distribution across dimensions and modalities.
> 
> **Re...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incremental-Knowledge-Model|Incremental Knowledge Model]]*

> [!definition] **Incremental Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that ability is malleable — capable of development through effort, strategy, and learning. Under incremental theory, performance outcomes are *feedback about current level of competence*, not diagnostic verdicts about fixed traits. Failure is informative rather than definitional; effort is a path to growth rather than evidence of low ability. Incremental theorists tend to seek challenge, persist in the face of difficulty, and process negative feedback as useful data.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incremental-Theory|Incremental Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Incremental Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> Dweck's technical term for the belief that intelligence is malleable and develops through effort. The theoretical basis for growth mindset interventions. This report finds the evidence for its cross-domain stability and its causal independence from experience to be weak.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incremental-Theory-of-Intelligence|Incremental Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Individual Interest** **[perm-note]**
> A relatively stable personal predisposition to re-engage with a particular class of content, objects, or activities, characterized by stored value (the content is found personally significant), stored knowledge (accumulated understanding of the domain), and positive affect (engagement is experienced as intrinsically rewarding). Individual interest is distinguished from situational interest (triggered by environmental features of specific texts or contexts) by its relative context-independence: individual interest in a domain motivates seeking out domain-relevant material even in the absence...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Individual-Interest|Individual Interest]]*

> [!definition] **Individualism-Collectivism and Attribution** **[perm-note]**
> **Individualistic** cultural frameworks — common in North American and Western European contexts — conceptualize the person as a discrete, autonomous agent whose behavior primarily reflects internal properties (traits, abilities, intentions, values). This cultural self-model primes dispositional attribution: when a person acts in a particular way, the natural causal search targets the person's internal properties. **Collectivistic** cultural frameworks — more common in East Asian, South Asian, and many African contexts — conceptualize the person as fundamentally embedded in relational, hier...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Individualism-Collectivism-and-Attribution|Individualism-Collectivism and Attribution]]*

> [!definition] **Inductive Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> A form of inference that moves from particular observations or instances to general conclusions, principles, or hypotheses. Unlike deductive reasoning, which guarantees its conclusions if its premises are true, inductive reasoning produces conclusions that are *probable* rather than *certain* given the evidence. The strength of an inductive argument is a matter of degree — it can be stronger or weaker — and even a maximally strong inductive argument can, in principle, be overturned by new evidence. Inductive reasoning is also called *ampliative* inference because the conclusion contains inf...
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inductive-Reasoning|Inductive Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Inert Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Inert knowledge refers to information that is acquired and stored but cannot be activated or deployed in novel problem contexts. It is the characteristic product of education organized around transmission of fixed content rather than around genuine inquiries. Pragmatist pedagogy aims specifically at the transformation of [[Prior-Knowledge|prior knowledge]] from inert reception to active competence — knowledge that functions as a tool for dealing with problems.
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inert-Knowledge|Inert Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Infallibilism** **[perm-note]**
> The contrasting view that genuine knowledge requires certainty — that a knower can only be said to *know* something if their justification is strong enough to exclude the possibility of error. On this view, if there is any chance (however remote) that a belief is false, it does not qualify as knowledge in the strict sense. [[Descartes]]'s method of doubt in the *Meditations* (1641) is the classical expression: he withholds the label "knowledge" from any belief that can be doubted. Many ordinary language uses of "know" implicitly invoke infallibilist standards.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Infallibilism]]*

> [!definition] **Inference** **[perm-note]**
> Inference is the cognitive act of drawing conclusions that go beyond what is explicitly stated, using available evidence and reasoning to arrive at justified beliefs. In education, inference involves identifying what follows from the evidence, recognising alternative interpretations, and understanding the degree of certainty warranted by the evidence at hand. Inference is not guessing — it is reasoned extension from known to unknown, constrained by the quality of available evidence.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Inference Generation in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> An inference is cognitive content that is activated during reading but is not explicitly stated in the text. Inference generation is the process by which readers go beyond the literal text to construct implicit meaning. Graesser, Singer, and Trabasso's constructionist theory distinguishes between two primary inference classes: *coherence inferences* (generated automatically to maintain local and global coherence of the text) and *elaborative inferences* (generated selectively to enrich the situation model with relevant world knowledge).
> 
> **Boundary:** Not all inferences are equally likely o...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / reading comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inference-Generation-in-Reading|Inference Generation in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Inference to the Best Explanation** **[perm-note]**
> The analytic philosophy reconstruction of Peircean abduction, understood as the inferential practice of selecting the hypothesis that, if true, would best explain the observed data according to criteria including explanatory power, fit with background knowledge, simplicity, and what Lipton calls "loveliness" (depth of illumination). IBE is simultaneously a description of how reasoners actually infer and a normative standard for how they should infer. Subject to the *bad lot problem* (van Fraassen): even the best available hypothesis may not be good in any absolute sense if the pool of candi...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inference-to-the-Best-Explanation|Inference to the Best Explanation]]*

> [!definition] **Inferential Bridging** **[perm-note]**
> The generation of implicit inferences needed to establish coherence between textual propositions that are not directly connected by explicit linguistic devices. A core component of situation model construction, requiring activation of world knowledge and causal reasoning. Inferential bridging demand is the primary determinant of text difficulty beyond the sentence level.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inferential-Bridging|Inferential Bridging]]*

> [!definition] **Information Transfer Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The view that formative assessment works by generating accurate data about a student's current knowledge state, and delivering that data to the student (and teacher) in a form that can be acted upon to close the learning gap. The operative mechanism is the quality and accessibility of the information.
> 
> **Core Assumption:** Students who receive accurate, timely, specific feedback will use it to adjust their learning behavior. The bottleneck is informational: if the right information is present, improvement follows.
> 
> **Design Implication:** Optimize assessment accuracy, feedba...
> 
> *Source: formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Information-Transfer-Model|Information Transfer Model]]*

> [!definition] **Information Transfer Model (ITM)** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The view that formative assessment works primarily through the delivery of accurate, timely, specific information about a student's performance gap. The operative variable is information quality and accessibility. **Boundary conditions:** not wrong, but insufficient; treats the monitoring system as a reliable conduit for information rather than as a variable in its own right.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The foil against which the MCM is developed. The ITM explains some variance but cannot explain the implementation gap or the differential effectiveness findings.
> 
> **Cr...
> 
> *Source: formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Information-Transfer-Model-ITM|Information Transfer Model (ITM)]]*

> [!definition] **The Information-Processing Foundation** **[perm-note]**
> The information-processing approach to cognition, which dominated [[cognitive-psychology]] from the late 1950s onward, treated the mind as a system that transforms, stores, and retrieves information according to computational principles. This framework imported concepts from communications engineering and computer science — channels, capacity limits, buffers, serial versus parallel processing — and applied them to the empirical study of human perception, attention, and memory. The first rigorous psychological application of these concepts to memory came from [[George-Miller]] (1956), who de...
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Information-Processing-Foundation|The Information-Processing Foundation]]*

> [!definition] **Informational Events** **[perm-note]**
> [**Informational-Events-Definition**:: External events whose primary functional significance is to provide genuine, non-pressuring information about the person's competence, mastery, or effectiveness. Examples include positive feedback framed as competence acknowledgment rather than evaluative pressure, optimal challenge calibration, and autonomy-supportive communication that conveys trust in the person's capacity. CET predicts that informational events maintain or enhance intrinsic motivation by affirming perceived competence within an autonomy-supportive context.]
> *See also: [[Information...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Informational-Events|Informational Events]]*

> [!definition] **Informational Feedback** **[perm-note]**
> Feedback whose primary functional significance is informational — it conveys genuine competence-relevant information to the recipient in a context where PLOC remains internal. Informational feedback is characterised by specificity (pointing to particular aspects of performance), mastery-referencing (comparing performance to personal standards or task standards rather than to other people), process orientation (attributing performance to strategies and effort rather than global ability), and non-controlling language (describing rather than evaluating). CET predicts that informational feedbac...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Informational-Feedback|Informational Feedback]]*

> [!definition] **Initial Mass Function** **[perm-note]**
> A statistical description of the distribution of stellar masses at the time of birth within a population of newly formed stars. The IMF describes the relative number of stars born in each mass range — it does not describe the mass of any individual star but rather the probability distribution from which individual stellar masses are drawn.
> 
> **Boundary:** The IMF is a population-level description, not a prediction for individual star-forming events. It is also a birth mass function — it does not account for mass lost during stellar evolution.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The IMF repres...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[Initial-Mass-Function|Initial Mass Function]]*

> [!definition] **Thinking Tag Semantics** **[perm-note]**
> **[Thinking-Tag-Semantics**:: XML tags (`<thinking>...</thinking>`) that mark content boundaries for differential processing - content within thinking tags treated as internal reasoning subject to logic optimization rather than presentation polish, exempt from brevity pressures, and hidden from standard user interfaces.]**
> 
> *Source: prompt-report-advanced-thinking-and-reasoning-tags-in-claude-202601070811* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Thinking-Tag-Semantics|Thinking Tag Semantics]]*

> [!definition] **Inner Speech** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Inner speech is the internalized, abbreviated, and predicate-dominant form of external speech that serves the self-regulatory function of mediating between thought and action. Distinguished from verbal thinking (internal monologue with full sentence structure) and from subvocalization (motor-level phonological encoding), inner speech in [[Vygotsky]]'s account is functionally social — it is internalized dialogue — and carries the compressed semantic architecture of the social speech from which it was derived. **Boundary condition**: inner speech is not the same as *all* menta...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inner-Speech|Inner Speech]]*

> [!definition] **Inoculation Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A framework for building cognitive resistance to misinformation, modeled on medical vaccination. Pre-emptive exposure to weakened forms of misinformation techniques — accompanied by explicit identification of the persuasion tactic employed — generates cognitive "antibodies" that reduce susceptibility to subsequent misinformation encounters. Inoculation theory grounds "prebunking" interventions, which aim to expose manipulation tactics before they are used, rather than "debunking" interventions, which correct misinformation after belief has formed.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inoculation-Theory|Inoculation Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Inquiry-Based Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A family of pedagogical approaches in which students are the primary agents of inquiry: they formulate or are presented with questions, generate hypotheses, design or conduct investigations, and draw conclusions from evidence. IBL is grounded philosophically in Dewey's *How We Think* (1910) and developed empirically through decades of science education research. In its most abductive instantiation, IBL deliberately creates anomalies and withholds pre-formed explanations to ensure that students engage in genuine hypothesis generation rather than simply verifying known outcomes.
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inquiry-Based-Learning|Inquiry-Based Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Instance Theory Core Mechanism** **[perm-note]**
> Instance theory proposes that each encounter with a stimulus creates a separate memory trace (instance) that can be retrieved in response to subsequent encounters. With sufficient practice, memory retrieval of solutions from past instances becomes faster than executing the algorithm de novo, leading to a race between algorithmic processing and memory retrieval. When memory consistently wins this race, performance appears automatic.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-attentional-efficiency-and-skill-aquisition* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Instance-Theory-Core-Mechanism|Instance Theory Core Mechanism]]*

> [!definition] **Instructional Efficiency Ratio** **[perm-note]**
> Proposed reconceptualization of germane load as a ratio: the proportion of total cognitive investment devoted to element interactivity at an appropriate expertise level. Replaces "increase germane load" with "optimize the instructional efficiency ratio" as the design prescription.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: cognitive-science/learning-theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[Instructional-Efficiency-Ratio|Instructional Efficiency Ratio]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated Cognitive Personhood** **[perm-note]**
> The emergent insight of Report 15: the claim that the examined life does not add cognitive capabilities to a pre-existing person but constitutes personhood in its fullest expression. When the fifteen dimensions of the Architecture of the Examined Life framework operate concurrently and fluidly — inquiry activation, fallibilist epistemology, autonomous motivation, constructive attentiveness, intellectual virtue, metacognitive governance, self-directed development, embodied awareness, emotional calibration, practical wisdom, social epistemic virtue, predictive flexibility, and narrative coher...
> 
> *Source: report-15-the-examined-life-revisited* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integrated-Cognitive-Personhood|Integrated Cognitive Personhood]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The most autonomous form of extrinsic motivation, in which identified goals have been synthesized with the coherent self-structure and other central personal values. [[Integrated-Regulation-(Motivation)]] shares many qualities with intrinsic motivation but differs in that the activity serves values or goals the person endorses rather than being inherently interesting in itself. Integration is most fully developed in mature adults and is associated with stable, conflict-free regulation of the broadest range of personally endorsed values.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integrated-Regulation|Integrated Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The most autonomous form of extrinsic motivation. The person has assimilated the identified value into a coherent sense of self — the activity is pursued not merely because it is valued in isolation but because it coheres with the person's broader values, goals, and sense of identity. Integration differs from identification in its *systemic* character: identified regulation can be isolated and context-specific, while integrated regulation is woven into the self-concept as a whole. Integration is associated with the best outcomes short of intrinsic motivation and, unlike intrinsic motivation...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integrated-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Integration** **[perm-note]**
> The fullest form of internalization, in which an externally-sourced regulation has been fully assimilated into the person's coherent sense of self — their identity, values, and other regulations. Integration is not merely endorsing a value as important (that is identification); it is the synthesis of that value with the whole person. Integrated values do not feel like additions to the self; they feel like expressions of it.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integration]]*

> [!definition] **Integration Anchor** **[perm-note]**
> The prior knowledge schema or structure that provides the connection point for situation model integration. When a reader encounters a text proposition and constructs a situation model, they are attaching the proposition to an existing schematic structure — the integration anchor — that provides the causal-relational context in which the proposition becomes meaningful.
> 
> **Boundary:** Integration anchors are not simply related prior knowledge. They must provide causal or relational structures into which the new proposition can be meaningfully connected. Loosely related information does not s...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integration-Anchor|Integration Anchor]]*

> [!definition] **Integration-Paradox** **[perm-note]**
> The structural paradox within OIT in which genuine identification with an externally sourced regulation requires a prior base of felt autonomy — the very resource that controlled motivational environments deplete. This paradox implies that motivation change cannot begin at the internalization step but must first attend to autonomy restoration.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integration-Paradox]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual Humility** **[perm-note]**
> An intellectual virtue consisting in the accurate recognition of one's epistemic limitations, susceptibility to error, and dependence on others for knowledge. Intellectual humility is the dispositional correlate of fallibilism: if we could be wrong, intellectual integrity demands that we hold beliefs with appropriate tentativeness, acknowledge the force of opposing arguments, and remain genuinely open to revision. Distinct from intellectual timidity (excessive self-doubt) and intellectual arrogance (unwarranted certainty).
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intellectual-Humility|Intellectual Humility]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual Standards** **[perm-note]**
> A set of criteria — clarity, accuracy, precision, relevance, depth, breadth, logic, significance, and fairness — used to evaluate the quality of reasoning. Applying intellectual standards to the elements of reasoning (purpose, question, information, inference, concepts, assumptions, implications, perspective) constitutes the core act of critical evaluation in the Paul-Elder framework. The standards are domain-transferable, though their application requires domain knowledge.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intellectual-Standards|Intellectual Standards]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual Virtues** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Intellectual Virtues ([[Jason-Baehr|Baehr]], 2011; [[Heather-Battaly|Battaly]], 2015; [[Linda-Zagzebski|Zagzebski]], 1996)
> **Definition:** Character traits that make their possessor an excellent epistemic agent — traits that reliably contribute to the formation and maintenance of true beliefs and the avoidance of error. Include [[Intellectual-Humility]], [[Intellectual-Courage]], [[Intellectual-Empathy]], [[Intellectual-Integrity]], and [[Intellectual-Perseverance]].
> **Boundary conditions:** Intellectual virtues can conflict (e.g., intellectual courage vs. intellectual humility); ...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intellectual-Virtues|Intellectual Virtues]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual-Standards** **[perm-note]**
> Evaluative criteria for reasoning quality, including clarity, accuracy, precision, relevance, depth, breadth, logic, significance, and fairness. Significance: transforms descriptive analysis into normative appraisal.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intellectual-Standards]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual-Traits** **[perm-note]**
> Dispositional virtues required for strong-sense critical thinking, such as [[Intellectual-Humility]], [[Intellectual-Courage]], and fair-mindedness. Significance: links reasoning quality to character development.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intellectual-Traits]]*

> [!definition] **Intention-Action Gap** **[perm-note]**
> **Intention-Action Gap**: The systematic failure of sincerely held behavioral intentions to translate into corresponding actions. Not a motivational deficit but an architectural one: the absence of specific cognitive and volitional structures (implementation intentions, action orientation, habit cues) that bridge aspiration and execution. Central to understanding why SRL cannot be reduced to motivation alone.
> See also: [[Implementation-Intention]], [[Self-Regulated-Learning]], [[Volitional-Control]]
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intention-Action-Gap|Intention-Action Gap]]*

> [!definition] **Interacting Elements** **[perm-note]**
> [**Interacting-Elements**:: Elements that cannot be understood in isolation — understanding any one element requires simultaneous consideration of its relationships to other elements. The number of interacting elements that must be simultaneously processed defines the level of element interactivity. Example: Understanding $F = ma$ requires simultaneously processing the concepts of force, mass, and acceleration, plus their multiplicative relationship.]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Interacting-Elements|Interacting Elements]]*

> [!definition] **Interaction Principle** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** [[Dewey's-Continuity-and-Interaction-Principles|Interaction Principle]] (Dewey, 1938)
> **Definition:** The principle that experience is always the product of the transaction between internal conditions (the learner's dispositions, knowledge, motivations, and purposes) and external conditions (the physical, social, and intellectual environment). Genuine education requires attending to both simultaneously.
> **Boundary Conditions:** Neither purely learner-centered nor purely environment-centered approaches satisfy the interaction principle — both poles must be addressed in their dynami...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[Interaction-Principle|Interaction Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Interleaving Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that mixing different problem types, topics, or subject matters within a study session produces substantially better performance on delayed tests and transfer problems than presenting all instances of one type before moving to the next (blocked practice), despite blocked practice producing better within-session performance.
> 
> **Boundary:** The interleaving advantage is largest for discriminable categories with overlapping surface features; it requires sufficient prior familiarity with each category to make discrimination a cognitively meaningful operation rather than random guess...
> 
> *Source: strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: learning science / pkb design*
> *Permanent Note: [[Interleaving-Effect|Interleaving Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Internalisation** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which externally regulated behaviours are gradually taken in and re-regulated by the self — moving along the self-determination continuum from external regulation toward identified regulation and ultimately integrated regulation. While CET specifically addresses intrinsic motivation (the far end of the intrinsic pole), Organismic Integration Theory (another SDT sub-theory) addresses internalisation of extrinsic motivation. The two processes are related: creating conditions that support autonomy and competence facilitates both intrinsic motivation (when activities are inherent...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalisation]]*

> [!definition] **Internalism** **[perm-note]**
> The position that the intrinsic, physical properties of a system's internal states — specifically, states inside the skin or skull of the organism — are both necessary and sufficient to determine the content and character of mental states. Internalism is the target of both content externalism (Putnam, Burge) and vehicle externalism (Clark, Chalmers). NOT the same as claiming that nothing external is causally relevant to cognition — only that the cognitive system itself is internally bounded.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalism]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization** **[perm-note]**
> The broad process of taking in a value, rule, or regulation from the social environment and making it one's own to varying degrees. Internalization spans the entire range from introjection to integration — it is the overarching process, not a single outcome. One can internalize a value incompletely (introjection), partially (identification), or fully (integration).
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalization]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> SDT's model of how external regulation is progressively taken over by internal regulatory processes, from external regulation (behaviour driven by external rewards/punishments) through introjection (partially internalised but controlled by internal pressure/guilt), identification (valued and self-endorsed), integration (fully assimilated into the self), to intrinsic motivation (activity is intrinsically rewarding). The reconstruction treats this as an empirically supported graded ordering while contesting its characterisation as a strict continuum with intrinsic motivation as its endpoint.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalization-Continuum|Internalization Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **The Internalization Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> [[Organismic-Integration-Theory]] describes how externally prompted behavior can be taken in and made progressively more self-determined. The continuum runs from [[External-Regulation|external regulation]] (compliance to obtain reward or avoid punishment) through [[Introjected-Regulation|introjected regulation]] (taking in but not accepting external pressure; ego-involvement, "should") to [[Identified-Regulation|identified regulation]] (endorsing the value of an activity even if not intrinsically enjoyable) to [[Integrated-Regulation|integrated regulation]] (full congruence of the activity'...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Internalization-Continuum|The Internalization Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization-Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> The progression from external regulation through introjection, identification, and integration to intrinsic motivation — not a spectrum of motivation amounts but a spectrum of degrees of integration between a regulatory process and the self-system. Position on the continuum reflects both the phenomenological locus of causality and the degree of structural integration of the regulation into the self.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalization-Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Interpretation and Inference** **[perm-note]**
> [**Inference**:: is the intellectual act of drawing conclusions from information and reasoning.] [**Interpretation**:: is the process of making sense of information by placing it within conceptual frameworks.] Together, they represent the *active cognitive work* of reasoning—<span style='color: #ff6e00;'>moving from premises to conclusions, from data to understanding, from observations to explanations</span>. Inference answers: *What conclusions am I drawing? How am I interpreting this information?*
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-fundamental-structures-of-reasoning-202512050320* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Interpretation-and-Inference|Interpretation and Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Interstellar Medium** **[perm-note]**
> The matter and radiation that exist in the space between star systems within a galaxy. Composed primarily of hydrogen (~70% by mass) and helium (~28%), with trace amounts of heavier elements and microscopic dust grains, the ISM exists in multiple phases — hot ionised gas, warm neutral gas, cold neutral gas, and molecular gas — each characterised by different temperatures, densities, and physical behaviours.
> 
> **Boundary:** The ISM does not include the circumstellar material gravitationally bound to individual stars, nor does it include the intergalactic medium between galaxies.
> 
> **Report-Spe...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[Interstellar-Medium|Interstellar Medium]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Cognitive Load** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive load imposed by the inherent complexity of the material to be learned, determined by the number and degree of interconnection among elements that must be simultaneously processed. Intrinsic load cannot be reduced by instructional design without changing the material itself or changing the learner's prior knowledge (which reduces element interactivity by automating some elements as schemas). For a given learner at a given expertise level, intrinsic load is essentially fixed. This is the "necessary" load of learning.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Cognitive-Load|Intrinsic Cognitive Load]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Cognitive Load in PKM** *[needs-note]*
> [**Intrinsic-Cognitive-Load-in-PKM**:: The cognitive demand imposed by the inherent complexity and element interactivity of the knowledge domain being studied, which cannot be reduced through PKM system design but can be managed through sequencing, chunking, and progressive schema construction.]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Cognitive-Load-in-PKM|Intrinsic Cognitive Load in PKM]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Goal Content** **[perm-note]**
> The four canonical intrinsic goal domains are: (1) **Personal growth** — developing oneself, learning new things, increasing self-understanding and psychological depth; (2) **Meaningful relationships** — building deep, authentic, mutually supportive connections with others; (3) **Community contribution** — working to make a positive difference in one's broader community or society; and (4) **Physical health and fitness** — maintaining and developing one's physical vitality and well-being. What unites these four domains, according to GCT, is that pursuing them tends to directly satisfy the b...
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Goal-Content|Intrinsic Goal Content]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> The motivation to engage in an activity for its inherent interest and enjoyment, rather than for separable external rewards or to avoid punishment. Intrinsically motivated learners are characterised by spontaneous curiosity, willingness to take on challenges, and sustained engagement. SDT posits that intrinsic motivation is neither rare nor fragile by nature — it is the natural state of healthy psychological functioning — but it is highly sensitive to the social conditions in which learning occurs, including, critically, the design and delivery of feedback.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Motivation|Intrinsic Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic-Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> Engagement driven by inherent satisfaction in the activity itself — the activity is its own reward. This is the form of motivation that first drove Deci and Ryan's research program, and it remains the paradigm case of self-determined motivation. However, [[Cognitive-Evaluation-Theory]] established that intrinsic motivation is fragile: it can be undermined by the wrong kinds of external events. This fragility is central to understanding the practical implications of the continuum.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjected Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory style in which the learner is driven by internalized pressures: guilt, shame, ego-threat, or the need to maintain contingent self-esteem. Introjected regulation is classified as controlled even though the source of pressure is internal, because the learner is not freely choosing engagement — they are responding to internal compulsions that feel obligatory rather than chosen. Students who study primarily to avoid feeling like a failure are exhibiting introjected regulation.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Introjected-Regulation|Introjected Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjected-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Behavior regulated by partially internalized external constraints, now represented *within* the person as ego-involving demands. The person acts to avoid shame, protect [[Self-Esteem|self-esteem]], or maintain conditional approval — but these demands are experienced as pressures, not as genuine expressions of the self. Introjection is the theoretical lynchpin of the internalization analysis: it is the site where external regulation enters the person but does not become part of the self. It is internal in location but external in phenomenological character.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Introjected-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjection** **[perm-note]**
> A regulatory style in which an external rule or value has been taken in but not genuinely made one's own — the person regulates themselves through internalized approval-seeking or shame-avoidance. Introjected regulation is internal in *location* (the pressure comes from within) but external in *quality* (its source is an internalized critic rather than a genuine value). Associated with ego-involvement, contingent self-esteem, performance anxiety, and — counterintuitively — psychological costs that may exceed those of external regulation.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Introjection]]*

> [!definition] **Hippocampal Reinstatement** **[perm-note]**
> The phenomenon, documented via fMRI, whereby the pattern of neural activity during successful memory retrieval recapitulates the pattern present during the original encoding episode. The degree of reinstatement correlates with the quality and richness of recollective experience. Provides neural-level support for the ESP's cue-engram resonance model.
> 
> **Boundary:** Reinstatement is a correlate of successful retrieval, not its full explanation. The mechanism by which reinstatement is achieved (hippocampal pattern completion) is an active area of investigation. The relationship between reinsta...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Hippocampal-Reinstatement|Hippocampal Reinstatement]]*

> [!definition] **Isolated Elements** **[perm-note]**
> [**Isolated-Elements**:: Elements that can be learned independently of one another, without reference to other elements. Each element can be processed serially in working memory, one at a time. Example: Vocabulary items in a foreign language, where knowing the word for "cat" does not require simultaneously knowing the word for "dog."]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Isolated-Elements|Isolated Elements]]*

## _J

> [!definition] **Jeans Instability** **[perm-note]**
> The condition under which a region of self-gravitating gas becomes unstable to gravitational collapse, occurring when the region's mass exceeds the Jeans mass — the critical mass at which self-gravity overwhelms thermal pressure. The Jeans mass depends on the temperature and density of the gas: lower temperatures and higher densities reduce the threshold, making collapse easier.
> 
> **Boundary:** The Jeans criterion applies to a simplified system (uniform, non-rotating, non-magnetised gas) and must be modified to account for turbulence, magnetic fields, and rotation in realistic astrophysical ...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[Jeans-Instability|Jeans Instability]]*

> [!definition] **Judgment of Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A metacognitive judgment made during or after study assessing how well particular material has been learned and how likely it is to be remembered later. JOLs are the most extensively studied metacognitive monitoring judgment. Delayed JOLs (made after a brief delay from study) are more accurate than immediate JOLs because they require actual retrieval rather than relying on current processing fluency as a proxy for memory.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Judgment-of-Learning|Judgment of Learning]]*

> [!definition] **The Judgment of Learning (JOL) Problem** **[perm-note]**
> Judgments of Learning are the metacognitive assessments learners make about how well they have learned material — "I understand this," "I could explain this," "I would get this right on a test." Research consistently shows that JOLs made immediately after studying are systematically overconfident, particularly for material studied through passive reading. Re-reading a passage increases fluency, and learners confuse fluency with understanding. Generative strategies — particularly self-testing and self-explanation — tend to produce more accurate JOLs because they require the learner to actual...
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Judgment-of-Learning-JOL-Problem|The Judgment of Learning (JOL) Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Judgment of Learning — JOL** *[needs-note]*
> A [[Judgment-of-Learning|judgment of learning]] is a metacognitive monitoring event in which the learner assesses the likelihood that a recently studied item will be successfully recalled at a future test. JOLs can be made immediately after study (immediate JOLs) or after a delay (delayed JOLs). The accuracy of JOLs depends critically on the cue basis: immediate JOLs rely on short-term memory cues and tend to be less accurate; delayed JOLs rely on retrieval attempts from long-term memory and are substantially more accurate.
> 
> **Boundary:** JOLs are prospective judgments about *future* perfor...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Judgment-of-Learning-—-JOL|Judgment of Learning — JOL]]*

## _K

> [!definition] **Knowing-That / Knowing-How** **[perm-note]**
> Gilbert Ryle's distinction between two types of knowledge: *knowing-that* (propositional knowledge: knowing that Paris is the capital of France) and *knowing-how* (procedural knowledge: knowing how to ride a bicycle). Ryle argued that knowing-how is not reducible to knowing-that — you cannot derive skilled performance from a set of propositions, however complete. This distinction is directly relevant to the tacit knowledge problem: expert skill represents knowing-how that cannot be converted into knowing-that without loss.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowing-That-Knowing-How|Knowing-That / Knowing-How]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge of Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> **Knowledge of Cognition** encompasses three subtypes of metacognitive knowledge that inform strategic deployment decisions. This represents the "database" that metacognitive control queries when selecting strategies.
> 
> **Declarative Knowledge**: Awareness of yourself as a learner—your strengths ("I excel at spatial reasoning"), weaknesses ("I struggle with probabilistic thinking"), and preferences ("I need concrete examples before abstraction"). This self-model enables resource pre-allocation: if you know probability is a weakness, you budget extra time and deploy verification strategies wh...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-of-Cognition|Knowledge of Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Telling** **[perm-note]**
> A writing process in which the writer retrieves and transcribes existing knowledge without encountering resistance between the content knowledge and the rhetorical demands of the writing situation. Produces fluent output without reorganising the underlying knowledge representation.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Telling|Knowledge Telling]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Telling vs. Knowledge Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> The foundational distinction in composition research between two fundamentally different writing processes. *Knowledge telling* is the retrieval and transcription of existing knowledge representations — the writer asks "what do I know about this?" and writes what comes to mind. *Knowledge transforming* involves a dialectical interaction between the writer's content knowledge and their rhetorical problem space — writing to an audience, for a purpose, under constraints that force the writer to evaluate, reorganize, and sometimes revise what they believe. Knowledge transforming cannot occur wi...
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Telling-vs.-Knowledge-Transforming|Knowledge Telling vs. Knowledge Transforming]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> A writing process in which the writer's content knowledge is pressed against the rhetorical demands of the writing situation, producing a dialectical interaction that reorganises understanding. Requires some form of communicative purpose that the writer's current knowledge cannot straightforwardly satisfy.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Transforming|Knowledge Transforming]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge-Activation Gap [Original Construct — Speculative Synthesis]** *[needs-note]*
> The systematic failure of stored, declaratively accurate metacognitive knowledge to activate in the relevant regulatory contexts during task performance, even when monitoring conditions that should trigger activation are present. Hypothesized to result from three contributing mechanisms: indexing insufficiency (declarative knowledge indexed to instruction contexts rather than task-embedded monitoring signals), proceduralization deficit (insufficient supported practice to proceduralize declarative knowledge), and monitoring-knowledge decoupling (weak associative links between monitoring stat...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Activation-Gap-Original-Construct-—-Speculative-Synthesis|Knowledge-Activation Gap [Original Construct — Speculative Synthesis]]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge-Telling** **[perm-note]**
> A writing strategy in which the writer retrieves and transcribes information already present in working memory or available in external sources, without significantly transforming, reorganising, or extending their understanding. The output accurately reflects pre-existing knowledge; the process adds nothing to it. Novice writers and most conventional note-taking operate in this mode.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Telling]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge-Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> A writing strategy in which the compositional process itself generates new understanding — through the need to resolve contradictions, fill explanatory gaps, reformulate confusing ideas, or integrate concepts that did not previously connect in the writer's mental model. Expert writers move between the knowledge space and the rhetorical space in a recursive dialogue that transforms both. Learning is a byproduct of this dialectic.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Transforming]]*

> [!definition] **Krashen's Input Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The theoretical claim that language acquisition — including vocabulary acquisition — occurs through exposure to *comprehensible input* slightly beyond the learner's current level (*i+1*). On this view, explicit instruction plays a minor role; the primary acquisition mechanism is the processing of large quantities of meaningful, comprehensible input. Applied to vocabulary, the hypothesis predicts that extensive reading is the primary and most efficient vocabulary acquisition mechanism.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The proximate theoretical source of the received view un...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Krashen's-Input-Hypothesis|Krashen's Input Hypothesis]]*

## _L

> [!definition] **Elaboration** **[perm-note]**
> Within the semantic processing level, the degree of richness, complexity, and associative spread with which a stimulus is encoded. More elaborated encodings recruit more prior knowledge nodes and create more retrieval pathways. Distinct from processing depth: elaboration modulates trace durability *within* a level, while depth determines the *class* of processing (structural, phonemic, semantic).
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaboration]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Encoding** **[perm-note]**
> A memory encoding strategy in which new information is processed by relating it to existing knowledge — generating connections, examples, questions, and explanatory structures. Elaborative encoding produces richer and more retrievable memory traces than maintenance encoding (rehearsal) or perceptual encoding (surface-level attention). It is the mechanism underlying the levels-of-processing effect.
> 
> **Boundary:** Elaborative encoding requires that existing knowledge exist to connect to. In genuinely novel domains with no relevant prior schemas, elaborative encoding may not be possible and ba...
> 
> *Source: pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-Encoding|Elaborative Encoding]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Inference** **[perm-note]**
> An inference generated during reading that goes beyond what is necessary for local text coherence, adding information from the reader's prior knowledge to enrich the mental model. Distinct from bridging inferences (necessary for coherence) and evaluative inferences (assessing text quality or truth). Elaborative inferences require cognitive resources and constructive engagement.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-Inference|Elaborative Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Interrogation** **[perm-note]**
> A prior knowledge activation strategy in which learners are prompted to answer "Why is this true?" or "How does this make sense given what I know?" for each new fact or proposition encountered. Distinguished from advance organizers by occurring *during* rather than *before* instruction, and by requiring active integration rather than providing scaffolding.
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-Interrogation|Elaborative Interrogation]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Rehearsal** **[perm-note]**
> Processing that increases depth by connecting a stimulus to existing knowledge, generating implications, creating associative links, or embedding it in meaningful context. In contrast to maintenance rehearsal, elaborative rehearsal moves processing down (deeper) the levels-of-processing continuum and produces meaningfully improved long-term retention. This distinction between the two types of rehearsal is one of LOP's most practically significant contributions.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-Rehearsal|Elaborative Rehearsal]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Richness** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a generated why-answer specifies a precise, mechanistic, and well-differentiated causal account, as contrasted with vague, tautological, or general explanatory claims. Elaborative richness is the within-deep-processing variable that determines the magnitude of EI's retention benefit: richer elaborations produce superior retention, particularly on transfer and inference measures.
> 
> **Boundary:** Elaborative richness is not merely answer length. A short, precise mechanistic account may be more elaboratively rich than a verbose but vague explanation.
> 
> **Research Status:** Th...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-Richness|Elaborative Richness]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative-Inference|Elaborative Inference** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition:** An inference that goes beyond what the text explicitly states to elaborate the situation model with information derived from prior knowledge — for example, inferring that a character is angry because the text described them slamming a door. Boundary condition: elaborative inferences are not required for text-base construction but are necessary for rich situation model construction; they are not generated automatically by most readers.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The primary cognitive mechanism bridging the text base to the situation model; its absence is the proximate...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-InferenceElaborative-Inference|Elaborative-Inference|Elaborative Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Late Selection** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #27FF00;'>The theoretical position that selection operates **after** complete perceptual and semantic processing. All information is analyzed for meaning; only the most pertinent information enters conscious awareness and working memory. Unattended stimuli influence processing implicitly even without conscious recognition.</span>
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-evolution-of-selective-attention-theory-202512142019* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Late-Selection|Late Selection]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Contracts** **[perm-note]**
> Negotiated agreements between learner and facilitator specifying learning goals, strategies and resources, evidence of learning, and evaluation criteria. The primary pedagogical instrument for operationalising SDL process in formal educational settings, learning contracts externalise and make visible the planning and goal-setting activities that natural SDL performs implicitly. Effective learning contracts are genuinely negotiated rather than merely approved; require iterative revision as learning proceeds; and are evaluated against jointly agreed criteria rather than normative benchmarks. ...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Learning-Contracts|Learning Contracts]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Projects** **[perm-note]**
> A concept from Allen Tough's empirical research designating any deliberate effort lasting at least seven hours to gain and retain specific knowledge or skills. Research using structured interviews found that average adults undertake roughly five to ten learning projects per year, approximately seventy percent of which are self-planned rather than institutionally directed. Learning projects data established the empirical foundation for the claim that SDL is not merely an educational ideal but a description of how the majority of adult learning actually occurs.
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Learning-Projects|Learning Projects]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Session Overview** **[perm-note]**
> **Topic**: <% tp.frontmatter.learning_objective %>
> **Learning Stage**: <% tp.frontmatter.learning_stage %>
> **Comprehension Level**: <% "⭐".repeat(tp.frontmatter.comprehension_level) %>
> **Quality**: <% tp.frontmatter.conversation_quality %>
> 
> *Source: reference-technical-advanced-templater-note-templates-20251118225816* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Learning-Session-Overview|Learning Session Overview]]*

> [!definition] **Element** **[perm-note]**
> [**Element-in-CLT**:: An element is anything that has been or needs to be learned — any unit of information that must be processed in working memory. For a novice, elements are typically the smallest meaningful components of the learning material. For an expert, a schema that encapsulates multiple lower-level elements functions as a single element.]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Element]]*

> [!definition] **Element Interactivity** **[perm-note]**
> A property of instructional material describing the degree to which its component elements must be processed simultaneously rather than sequentially for understanding to occur. High element interactivity material (e.g., understanding how grammar rules interact to produce a complex sentence; understanding how enzyme kinetics depends on simultaneous knowledge of substrate concentration, inhibitor presence, and pH) imposes high intrinsic cognitive load because the elements cannot be meaningfully separated. Low element interactivity material (e.g., memorizing vocabulary definitions, learning a ...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Element-Interactivity|Element Interactivity]]*

> [!definition] **Elements of Thought** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Elements of Thought ([[Richard-Paul|Paul]] & [[Linda-Elder|Elder]], 2001)
> **Definition:** Eight universal structures present in all reasoning: purpose, question at issue, information, interpretation/inference, concepts, assumptions, implications/consequences, and point of view. Critical analysis involves examining each element for quality using Intellectual Standards.
> **Boundary conditions:** The framework provides structural analysis tools but requires domain knowledge for meaningful application. Not all reasoning tasks engage all eight elements equally.
> **Report significance:** ...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elements-of-Thought|Elements of Thought]]*

> [!definition] **Elenchus** **[perm-note]**
> The Socratic method of cross-examination: a systematic procedure in which the Questioner draws out the interlocutor's commitments through apparently innocent questions, then demonstrates that those commitments, taken together, generate a contradiction or inadequacy. The elenchus does not refute from the outside; it shows the interlocutor that their own beliefs are internally inconsistent. Vlastos distinguishes the *elenchus* (which aims at exposing contradictions) from the *maieutic method* (which aims at helping the interlocutor give birth to insight).
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elenchus]]*

> [!definition] **Teleological Directionality** **[perm-note]**
> The implicit or explicit assumption in a developmental theory that development has a natural endpoint toward which it tends under appropriate conditions. In OIT, the teleological directionality is the assumption that internalization naturally tends toward integration. Philosophically, teleological directionality in developmental theories should be examined for: (1) the degree to which the "natural endpoint" is empirically demonstrated vs. normatively prescribed; (2) whether the endpoint reflects universal human development or the theorist's cultural context; and (3) whether the directionali...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Teleological-Directionality|Teleological Directionality]]*

> [!definition] **Levels of Processing** **[perm-note]**
> A theoretical framework proposing that stimuli can be analyzed at multiple levels of abstraction — from shallow physical/structural features to deep semantic/meaningful features — and that the level at which analysis occurs determines the durability of the resulting memory trace. "Deeper" processing, involving analysis of meaning and semantic associations, produces stronger, more durable traces than "shallower" processing focused on physical features. Importantly, this does not require more time — it requires different *kinds* of cognitive operations.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Levels-of-Processing|Levels of Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Levels of Processing Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A framework proposing that the durability of memory traces is a function of the depth at which information is processed during encoding. Shallow processing (attending to sensory or perceptual features such as letter shapes or sounds) produces weaker, less durable traces. Deeper processing (attending to semantic, conceptual, and associative features) produces stronger, more durable traces. Elaborative rehearsal — actively connecting new information to existing knowledge — represents the deep end of the processing continuum.
> 
> **Boundary:** LOP has been criticized for circularity (defining dep...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Levels-of-Processing-Theory|Levels of Processing Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Lexical Automaticity** **[perm-note]**
> The state in which word recognition (mapping orthographic form to phonological and semantic representations) requires minimal attentional resources. Lexical automaticity is achieved through massive exposure to a word form across varied contexts. It is a graded property — recognition can be partially automatized — not a binary state.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Lexical-Automaticity|Lexical Automaticity]]*

> [!definition] **Lexical Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The multi-dimensional representation of a word in long-term memory, comprising at minimum four layers of knowledge that are acquired incrementally and through different types of exposure. Full lexical knowledge of a word includes knowing its phonological form, its orthographic form, its morphological family, its syntactic behavior, its primary and secondary meanings, its collocational patterns (what words it typically occurs near), its register constraints (formal vs. informal), and its conceptual referent. Importantly, these layers are not acquired simultaneously — reading may contribute d...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[Lexical-Knowledge|Lexical Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Lexical Quality** **[perm-note]**
> The precision and completeness of a word's representation in long-term memory, encompassing orthographic, phonological, morphological, semantic, and syntactic information. High-quality lexical representations are retrieved rapidly and automatically during reading, consuming minimal working memory. Low-quality representations require effortful processing that competes with comprehension.
> 
> **Boundary:** Lexical quality is a continuum, not a binary. The same word may have high-quality representation in one person's lexicon and low-quality in another's — even if both individuals would pass a vo...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[Lexical-Quality|Lexical Quality]]*

> [!definition] **Lexicon** **[perm-note]**
> 
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Lexicon]]*

> [!definition] **Lexicon: Core Terms** **[perm-note]**
> 1. **[[Cognitive-Load-Theory]]** — An instructional design theory based on the premise that [[Working-Memory]] has limited capacity, and that instructional materials must be designed to manage this constraint for effective [[Schema-Construction]].
> 
> 2. **[[Intrinsic-Cognitive-Load]]** — The portion of cognitive load attributable to the inherent complexity of the material being learned, determined by [[Element-Interactivity]] relative to the learner's existing [[schemas]].
> 
> 3. **[[Extraneous-Cognitive-Load]]** — The portion of cognitive load attributable to the design of the learning environm...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Lexicon-Core-Terms|Lexicon: Core Terms]]*

> [!definition] **Literature Review** **[perm-note]**
> A systematic literature review represents a comprehensive approach to identifying, selecting, and analyzing relevant literature on specific research questions through structured protocols that minimize bias while maximizing the validity and reliability of findings. Within [[Personal-Knowledge-Management]] systems, this definition expands to encompass not merely the production of a review document but the *integration of sources into a living knowledge network* where individual insights become reusable nodes supporting multiple future investigations.
> 
> *Source: pkb-report-literature-review-methodology-for-pkb-research-202512040139* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Literature-Review|Literature Review]]*
