> **Definition:** The vocabulary of self-direction exists on a spectrum corresponding to the [[Internalization-Continuum]] in [[Self-Determination-Theory]]: from *introjected regulatory language* ("I have to understand this or I'll feel like a failure") to *identified regulatory language* ("I want to understand this because it matters to my goals") to *integrated regulatory language* ("understanding this is part of what I do as a learner"). The locus and quality of causality encoded in the vocabulary signals the regulatory style of the inner speech.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This pro...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Language-Spectrum|Regulatory Language Spectrum]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Mode** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A fundamental dimension of motivated behavior distinguishing whether behavioral regulation is oriented toward achieving a desired positive outcome (approach) or toward preventing an undesired negative outcome (avoidance). NOT equivalent to the distinction between positive and negative affect — both approach and avoidance can involve positive or negative affect; the distinction is regulatory, not affective.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the primary analytic construct in this report. The regulatory mode primacy thesis claims that this dimension is more theoreticall...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Mode|Regulatory Mode]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Overhead** **[perm-note]**
> Original analytical concept developed in this report. The additional cognitive-affective burden imposed by controlled motivation (specifically introjected regulation) — the self-surveillance, performance anxiety, and contingent self-esteem management that compete with cognitive resources available for task engagement. Explains why autonomous motivation predicts not just greater effort but qualitatively deeper and more creative processing.
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Overhead|Regulatory Overhead]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Phases** **[perm-note]**
> Pintrich organizes self-regulatory activity into four broad phases that describe the temporal unfolding of a learning episode: (1) *Forethought, Planning, and Activation*, the anticipatory phase in which goals are set, prior knowledge activated, and strategies planned before task engagement; (2) *Self-Monitoring*, the metacognitive surveillance of one's own cognitive, motivational, behavioral, and contextual states during task execution; (3) *Self-Control and Regulation*, the deployment of specific regulatory interventions in response to monitoring signals; and (4) *Self-Reflection and Eval...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-model-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Phases|Regulatory Phases]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Poverty** **[perm-note]**
> A state in which a learner's strategic repertoire is insufficiently developed to provide adequate responses to monitoring signals. Even accurate monitoring coupled with threshold-crossing salience produces only generic, minimally effective regulatory responses when the repertoire is thin. Distinguished from monitoring failure and threshold insensitivity as a third independent source of coupling breakdown.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Poverty|Regulatory Poverty]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Quality** **[perm-note]**
> A dimension of motivation describing the degree to which a regulatory behavior is experienced as autonomous (originating from the self, aligned with values and interests) versus controlled (experienced as compelled by external forces or internal pressure). Regulatory quality exists on a continuum from external regulation through introjection, identification, and integration to intrinsic motivation, corresponding to progressively deeper internalization of motivational reasons. High regulatory quality is associated with sustained engagement, deep processing, and positive affect under difficul...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Quality|Regulatory Quality]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Quality in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> Regulatory quality refers to the degree to which a reader's engagement with a text is governed by autonomous reasons (finding the text genuinely interesting, valuing the knowledge for personal growth, experiencing deep immersion) versus controlled reasons (reading to avoid negative consequences, to demonstrate compliance, or to satisfy external pressure). Autonomous regulation and controlled regulation exist on a continuum in SDT's [[Organismic-Integration-Theory]], ranging from external regulation through introjection, identification, and integration toward intrinsic motivation.
> 
> **Boundar...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Quality-in-Reading|Regulatory Quality in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Reflexivity Problem** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The theoretical challenge created by Pintrich's inclusion of motivation as a regulated domain: if motivation is regulated by a regulatory executive, that executive must itself have some motivational basis — what motivates the motivation-regulator? The problem resolves not by positing a fifth unregulated level but by recognizing that regulatory activity is bootstrapped from dispositional motivational tendencies (trait self-efficacy, general mastery orientation) that are products of developmental history. See Phase VI Synthesis.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the r...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Reflexivity-Problem|Regulatory Reflexivity Problem]]*

> [!definition] **The Regulatory Reflexivity Problem** *[needs-note]*
> The regulatory reflexivity problem designates the structural challenge that metacognitive regulation, being itself a cognitive process, is subject to the same monitoring and control demands as any other cognitive process — generating an apparent regress in which the regulation of regulation would require further regulation, ad infinitum. The problem is not merely philosophical: it has practical manifestations in the difficulty of evaluating one's own regulatory effectiveness (evaluating evaluation), and in the tendency for regulatory processes to become themselves objects of meta-regulatory...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Regulatory-Reflexivity-Problem|The Regulatory Reflexivity Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Self-Talk** **[perm-note]**
> Inner speech that carries the regulatory register — the "why am I doing this" and "how am I directing this effort" layer of motivational inner speech. SDT's regulatory language research establishes that controlled regulatory vocabulary (should/must/have to) produces a functionally different motivational state than autonomous regulatory vocabulary (want/choose/find meaningful), even when describing identical behavior.
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Self-Talk|Regulatory Self-Talk]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Self-Talk Register** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The portion of inner speech that carries regulatory content — the "why am I doing this" and "how am I directing this effort" layer of motivational inner speech. Ranges from controlled regulatory language (should/must/have to) through introjected (guilt/shame avoidance), identified (personally meaningful), and integrated (identity-consistent) registers. Distinguished from attributional vocabulary (which explains past causes) and from evaluative vocabulary (which assesses current states) by its prospective and directive character.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The SDT Reg...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Self-Talk-Register|Regulatory Self-Talk Register]]*

> [!definition] **The Regulatory Spectrum** **[perm-note]**
> OIT proposes six regulatory styles arranged from least to most autonomous:
> 
> 1. **Amotivation** — absence of intentional regulation; the person does not act or acts without intention
> 2. **External Regulation** — behavior controlled by external rewards and punishments; fully controlled regulation
> 3. **Introjected Regulation** — regulation by internalized pressures: ego-involvement, guilt, shame, contingent self-worth; still controlled, but the controlling force is now internal
> 4. **Identified Regulation** — the person consciously values the goal and acts from that valuation; autonomous, thoug...
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Regulatory-Spectrum|The Regulatory Spectrum]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Style** **[perm-note]**
> The characteristic reason or perceived locus of causality underlying a given behavior. Regulatory styles describe *why* a person engages in an activity, ranging from pure external compulsion through various degrees of internalization to fully autonomous self-regulation. OIT is specifically concerned with the continuum of regulatory styles applied to *extrinsic* behaviors — those not initially intrinsically motivated.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Style|Regulatory Style]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Threshold** **[perm-note]**
> The minimum signal strength required to activate regulatory processes. Below the threshold, monitoring signals are registered but produce no behavioral change (the cognitive "dead band"). The threshold is modulated by motivational orientation, cognitive load, self-efficacy, and temporal discounting. See [[Monitoring-Control-Coupling]].
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Threshold|Regulatory Threshold]]*

> [!definition] **Relatedness** **[perm-note]**
> The need to feel securely connected to others, to experience care and belonging, and to feel that one matters to the people around them. Relatedness in SDT does not require close friendship or intimacy — it can be satisfied by a classroom culture where students feel respected and valued by their teacher and peers. The critical functional requirement is felt security: that one's standing in the relational environment is not contingent on performance.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relatedness]]*

> [!definition] **Relatedness Need** **[perm-note]**
> The need for [[Relatedness]] is the need to experience *genuine connection* with others — to feel that one belongs, that one matters to others, and that others matter to oneself. This is more than affiliation; it requires a sense of mutual care and significance rather than merely proximity or social contact. In educational contexts, the relatedness need encompasses connections to instructors, peers, and the broader learning community. Importantly, relatedness satisfaction does not require large social networks — a single deeply satisfying relational connection can substantially fulfill the ...
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relatedness-Need|Relatedness Need]]*

> [!definition] **Relational-Emergent Autonomy** **[perm-note]**
> The reconstruction's key novel concept: autonomous motivation as a property that emerges in the transaction between a goal-directed person and an autonomy-supportive social context, rather than as an internal biological need that contexts either satisfy or frustrate. Reframes intervention from "satisfy the need" to "create the transaction conditions in which autonomous motivation can emerge."
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relational-Emergent-Autonomy|Relational-Emergent Autonomy]]*

> [!definition] **Relationship Motivation Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The seventh mini-theory of Self-Determination Theory, developed by Deci and Ryan, which examines how the basic psychological needs for autonomy, competence, and relatedness operate within close personal relationships and how the need-support or need-thwarting character of a relationship determines both its quality and its effects on participants' broader well-being. RMT does NOT reduce to the claim that good relationships are beneficial; it specifies the motivational mechanisms that distinguish beneficial from harmful relationships.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relationship-Motivation-Theory|Relationship Motivation Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Relationship Motivation Theory — Core Definition** *[needs-note]*
> Relationship Motivation Theory (RMT) is the sixth mini-theory within Self-Determination Theory, proposing that people are motivated to pursue close relationships in qualitatively different ways — autonomously (because the relationships are genuinely valued and the other person matters intrinsically) or in a controlled fashion (because of guilt, obligation, fear of rejection, or contingent self-worth). RMT further proposes that perceived partner responsiveness — the degree to which one's partner is perceived to see, understand, validate, and care for one's authentic self — is the primary mec...
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relationship-Motivation-Theory-—-Core-Definition|Relationship Motivation Theory — Core Definition]]*

> [!definition] **Relative Autonomy Index** **[perm-note]**
> A composite measure of regulatory style derived by weighting individual regulation subscale scores: external and introjected regulations receive negative weights, identified and integrated regulations receive positive weights, intrinsic motivation receives the highest positive weight. The RAI produces a single continuous index from highly controlled to highly autonomous, operationalizing OIT's core dimension for research purposes. Subject to scoring convention debates in the literature.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relative-Autonomy-Index|Relative Autonomy Index]]*

> [!definition] **Relative Centrality Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> **Relative Centrality Hypothesis** — The GCT proposition that well-being is predicted not by the absolute level of any specific aspiration but by the *relative priority* of intrinsic over extrinsic aspirations in an individual's overall aspiration profile. A person can hold extrinsic aspirations without negative well-being consequences as long as those aspirations are subordinate to, rather than dominant over, intrinsic aspirations. See Sections 1, 2.
> **See also:** [[Aspiration-Index]], [[Goal-Contents-Theory]]
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relative-Centrality-Hypothesis|Relative Centrality Hypothesis]]*

> [!definition] **Repeated Reading** **[perm-note]**
> An evidence-based fluency intervention in which students read short passages multiple times until reaching a fluency criterion (typically a target WCPM score), with corrective feedback from a tutor or teacher. Samuels demonstrated that repeated reading produces not only fluency gains on practiced passages but generalization to unpracticed text, indicating that practice builds transferable decoding efficiency rather than merely word-specific memorization.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Repeated-Reading|Repeated Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Research Context** **[perm-note]**
> **Primary Question**: <% tp.frontmatter.research_question %>
> **Research Phase**: <% tp.frontmatter.research_phase %>
> **Discipline**: <% tp.frontmatter.academic_discipline %>
> **LLM Used**: <% tp.frontmatter.llm_model %> (<% tp.frontmatter.generation_date %>)
> 
> *Source: reference-technical-advanced-templater-note-templates-20251118225816* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Research-Context|Research Context]]*

> [!definition] **Research Question** **[perm-note]**
> **Primary Question**: <% tp.frontmatter.research_topic %>
> 
> **Sub-Questions:**
> 1. 
> 2. 
> 3.
> 
> *Source: reference-technical-advanced-templater-note-templates-20251118225816* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Research-Question|Research Question]]*

> [!definition] **The Threshold-Dependency Principle** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The structural property of acquisition systems in which the primary growth mechanism is conditionalised on prior acquisition that the mechanism itself cannot efficiently provide. Below the threshold, the primary mechanism is inefficient and a different acquisition strategy (typically more deliberate and instructional) is required; above it, the primary mechanism becomes self-reinforcing. Confusing an above-threshold mechanism for a universal one is a recurrent error in educational prescription.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The most precise formulation of the central ch...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Threshold-Dependency-Principle|The Threshold-Dependency Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Restructuring** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The process by which entirely new schemas are created, typically through: (a) *patterning* — inducing a new schema from regularities across experience, (b) *schema induction* — creating a new schema by abstracting a pattern from an analogous domain, or (c) *analogy* — building a new schema by structural mapping from an existing schema in a different domain.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** Restructuring is the closest Schema Theory comes to a genuine construction mechanism. It is, however, underspecified in Rumelhart & Norman's original account — the trigger conditions, the time co...
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Restructuring]]*

> [!definition] **Retrieval** **[perm-note]**
> Retrieval is the process by which stored memory representations are accessed, reactivated, and brought into conscious awareness (in the case of explicit retrieval) or expressed through changes in performance (in the case of implicit retrieval). Retrieval is not a passive "reading out" of stored information but an active, cue-dependent, reconstructive process that is shaped by the interaction between the retrieval cue, the stored trace, and the broader cognitive and environmental context.
> 
> **Boundary:** Retrieval failure does not necessarily indicate that a memory has been lost from storage;...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Retrieval]]*

> [!definition] **Retrieval Practice Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The robust finding that attempting to retrieve information from memory produces substantially better long-term retention than re-studying the same material for equivalent time, even when initial retrieval is difficult or incomplete. The benefit persists and often increases over longer retention intervals. Mechanisms proposed include strengthening of retrieval routes, elaborative encoding during effortful retrieval, and error-correction processes engaged by retrieval failure. A core instance of the broader principle of desirable difficulties. Practically, this means that low-stakes frequent ...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Retrieval-Practice-Effect|Retrieval Practice Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Retrieval-Induced Forgetting** **[perm-note]**
> The phenomenon whereby retrieval practice on a subset of studied items produces subsequent impairment of memory for related-but-not-practiced items, compared to items from an unrelated category that received no retrieval practice. Discovered by [[Robert-Bjork]] and colleagues. The mechanism is thought to involve inhibitory suppression: during retrieval, similar competitor items that produce interference are actively inhibited to reduce competition, and this inhibition persists beyond the retrieval episode. Retrieval-induced forgetting demonstrates that retrieval is not a passive read proces...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Retrieval-Induced-Forgetting|Retrieval-Induced Forgetting]]*

> [!definition] **Retrieval-Writing** **[perm-note]**
> Writing performed without access to source material, requiring the writer to retrieve from memory rather than transcribe from a source. Activates the testing effect mechanism and the articulatory construction mechanism simultaneously. Produces less fluent, less complete notes than note-taking — and, in most circumstances, more durable encoding.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Retrieval-Writing]]*

> [!definition] **Reward** **[perm-note]**
> The positive outcome — whether hedonic, social, or informational — that follows a routine and reinforces the cue-routine association through dopaminergic signaling. Crucially, in established habits, the *anticipation* of reward (craving) becomes as motivationally significant as reward receipt itself, creating the pull that makes habits self-sustaining.
> 
> **Boundary:** The reward need not be a conscious pleasure. Stress reduction, social belonging, or a sense of completion can all function as habit-maintaining rewards. Many destructive habits persist through negative reinforcement — the reduc...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Reward]]*

> [!definition] **Reward Contingency** *[needs-note]*
> Reward contingency refers to the relationship structure between a reward and the behavior or outcome for which it is delivered. CET distinguishes: task-noncontingent (reward independent of engagement), [[Engagement-Contingent-Reward|engagement-contingent]] (reward for engaging), completion-contingent (reward for finishing), and performance-contingent (reward for performing above a standard). The contingency structure determines, to a significant degree, the functional significance of the reward: engagement- and completion-contingent rewards are most reliably controlling; unexpected, task-no...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Reward-Contingency|Reward Contingency]]*

> [!definition] **Reward Contingency Types** **[perm-note]**
> The structural relationship between performance and reward. Three types matter most for CET: (1) **Task-contingent rewards** — given simply for participating in or completing the task, without reference to quality (e.g., "here is a sticker for drawing"). (2) **Completion-contingent rewards** — given for completing the task to a basic standard. (3) **Performance-contingent rewards** — given in proportion to quality of performance (e.g., "top marks get a prize"). CET predicts that task-contingent and completion-contingent rewards carry primarily a controlling functional significance and relia...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Reward-Contingency-Types|Reward Contingency Types]]*

> [!definition] **Hermeneutic Circle** **[perm-note]**
> The interpretive principle, derived from hermeneutic philosophy, that understanding a part of a text requires reference to the whole, and understanding the whole requires reference to the parts. Silent reading's recursive re-entry affordance operationalizes this principle cognitively, enabling the iterative revision of interpretation that constitutes genuine textual analysis.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Hermeneutic-Circle|Hermeneutic Circle]]*

> [!definition] **Routine** **[perm-note]**
> The behavioral sequence that constitutes the habitual response itself — the "middle term" of the habit loop. Routines may be simple (a single motor gesture) or complex (an extended behavioral chain). What unifies them is that the entire sequence is packaged as a single executable unit in memory, typically through the process of chunking in the [[Basal-Ganglia|basal ganglia]].
> 
> **See also:** [[Chunking]], [[Procedural-Memory]], [[Skill-Acquisition]]
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Routine]]*

> [!definition] **Routing Decision Factors** **[perm-note]**
> The main agent considers:
> 
> **1. Task complexity**:
> - Simple (single agent)
> - Moderate (2-3 agents sequentially)  
> - Complex (5+ agents, potentially parallel)
> 
> **2. Domain matching**:
> - Keywords in user query vs. agent descriptions
> - Trigger phrase alignment
> - Explicit user requests ("use X agent")
> 
> **3. Dependency analysis**:
> - Sequential: B depends on A's output
> - Parallel: A and B are independent
> - Conditional: C only needed if A finds issues
> 
> **4. Resource constraints**:
> - Token budget (prefer Haiku agents for volume)
> - Time sensitivity (prefer parallel for speed)
> - Quality requirements ...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Routing-Decision-Factors|Routing Decision Factors]]*

> [!definition] **Error Categories in Multi-Agent Systems** **[perm-note]**
> **1. Agent invocation errors**:
> - Agent not found (typo in name)
> - Agent unavailable (resource limits)
> - Tool permission denied
> 
> **2. Agent execution errors**:
> - Agent reaches token limit mid-task
> - Tool execution fails (file not found, command error)
> - Agent produces invalid output format
> 
> **3. Coordination errors**:
> - Circular dependencies between agents
> - Conflicting state updates
> - Deadlock (agents waiting on each other)
> 
> **4. Quality errors**:
> - Agent completes but output doesn't meet criteria
> - Security agent finds critical vulnerabilities
> - Test agent reports failures
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Error-Categories-in-Multi-Agent-Systems|Error Categories in Multi-Agent Systems]]*

> [!definition] **RSVP** **[perm-note]**
> A text display mode presenting words one at a time at a fixed rate, eliminating saccadic eye movements. Used in speed-reading applications and research. RSVP disables strategic rereading and comprehension-monitoring-driven gaze control, producing comprehension deficits relative to normal reading at equivalent word rates for complex text.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[RSVP]]*

> [!definition] **The Rubicon Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Rubicon Model of Action Phases** (Heckhausen & Gollwitzer): A four-phase account of how intentions progress to action — predecisional deliberation, postdecisional planning, actional behavior, and evaluative reflection. The "Rubicon" is the qualitative transition from open deliberation to committed implementation; failure to complete this transition is a primary mechanism of the intention-action gap.
> See also: [[Implementation-Intention]], [[Forethought-Phase]], [[Metacognitive-Regulation]]
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Rubicon-Model|The Rubicon Model]]*

> [!definition] **Rubric** **[perm-note]**
> A structured framework describing levels of performance on relevant criteria. In autonomy-supportive feedback design, rubrics serve primarily as a *metacognitive scaffold* — externalising the evaluative standards that learners must internalise in order to self-assess — rather than as a scoring device. Effective rubrics describe *qualitative differences* in performance at each level rather than accumulating points, and use language accessible to learners at the relevant developmental stage.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Rubric]]*

> [!definition] **Rumelhart's Schema Specification** **[perm-note]**
> A schema, in Rumelhart's formalization, is a data structure that represents a generalized concept. It consists of:
> 1. **A network of interrelated concepts** — the elements of the schema and their relationships
> 2. **Variable slots** — positions within the schema that can be filled by specific instances (variables, not constants)
> 3. **Default values** — default fillers for each slot, drawn from typical instances
> 4. **Constraints** — constraints on what can fill each slot
> 5. **Embedded sub-schemas** — other schemas that fill slots in the parent schema
> 
> A schema is instantiated when its variabl...
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Rumelhart's-Schema-Specification|Rumelhart's Schema Specification]]*

> [!definition] **Rumelhart's Technical Schema Specification** **[perm-note]**
> For Rumelhart, a schema is a data structure for representing generic concepts — not a fixed template but a packet of information that includes: (1) a set of variables (slots) representing attributes of the concept; (2) constraints on the values those variables can take; (3) default values (typical values assumed when specific information is unavailable); and (4) embedded sub-schemas representing constituent components. Crucially, Rumelhart specified that schemas are not passive stored memories but active *procedures* — they impose themselves on incoming information through a process of inst...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Rumelhart's-Technical-Schema-Specification|Rumelhart's Technical Schema Specification]]*

> [!definition] **Rumination vs. Metacognitive Reflection** **[perm-note]**
> Rumination and metacognitive reflection are functionally inverse processes that can be superficially confused. Both involve returning repeatedly to past performance or experience. They differ in: **temporal orientation** (rumination is retrospective and circular; reflection is retrospective as a means to prospective revision), **cognitive product** (rumination produces affective intensity without cognitive output; reflection produces metacognitive knowledge updates), **controllability** (rumination is experienced as intrusive and uncontrollable; reflection is voluntarily initiated and termi...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Rumination-vs.-Metacognitive-Reflection|Rumination vs. Metacognitive Reflection]]*

## _S

> [!definition] **Satisfaction-Frustration Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> The empirically established principle that need frustration and need absence are not points on the same continuum but qualitatively distinct states with different outcome profiles. Operationalized in the BPNSFS, which uses separate subscales for satisfaction and frustration of each need and shows that frustration predicts ill-being and controlled motivation above and beyond what is predicted by low satisfaction alone.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Satisfaction-Frustration-Asymmetry|Satisfaction-Frustration Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Satisfaction-Frustration-Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that need satisfaction and need frustration are not mirror images (endpoints of a single scale) but qualitatively distinct processes with different causal antecedents and consequence profiles. Need frustration produces active damage that cannot be fully remediated by restoring need satisfaction, constituting SDT as a theory of psychological damage as well as flourishing.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Satisfaction-Frustration-Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Scaffold Dependence** **[perm-note]**
> The failure mode in which a learner develops reliable performance in a scaffolded environment without developing the internal capacity to perform independently when the scaffold is removed. Scaffold dependence results from scaffolding that is too effective at reducing challenge, from insufficient fading, or from failure to transfer metacognitive strategies to conditions that lack the scaffolding's contextual cues.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Scaffold-Dependence|Scaffold Dependence]]*

> [!definition] **Scaffolded Fading** **[perm-note]**
> The planned, gradual withdrawal of scaffolding support as learner competence develops, ultimately requiring independent performance. [[Scaffolded-Fading]] is the mechanism by which scaffolding becomes a developmental rather than a permanent support. Without fading, scaffolding produces competence at performing tasks with support but not competence at the task itself. The timing and gradient of fading is one of the most important and underspecified aspects of scaffolding design.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Scaffolded-Fading|Scaffolded Fading]]*

> [!definition] **Scaffolded Inner Speech Template** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A pre-formulated vocabulary structure used to prime or guide inner speech at key SRL transition points — before reading (forethought), during reading (performance monitoring), and after reading (self-reflection). Templates are not scripts to be repeated verbatim but vocabulary scaffolds that provide the grammatical and semantic structures within which the reader can insert content-specific language. Effective templates encode the constitutive vocabulary principles: mastery orientation, autonomy-supportive framing, controllable-cause attribution, and temporal developmental fr...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Scaffolded-Inner-Speech-Template|Scaffolded Inner Speech Template]]*

> [!definition] **Scaffolding** **[perm-note]**
> Scaffolding is a form of adult-child interaction in which a more knowledgeable partner systematically adjusts their support to the learner's level of competence — providing maximal assistance at the outset and progressively withdrawing it as the learner acquires increasing independence, until the learner can perform the task or exercise the skill autonomously. The scaffold is not the learning; it is the temporary support structure that makes learning possible.
> 
> **Boundary:** Scaffolding does not refer to all forms of instructional support but specifically to contingent, fading support calib...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Scaffolding]]*

> [!definition] **Scaffolding — Instructional Design** **[perm-note]**
> Contingent support provided to a learner working within their [[Zone-of-Proximal-Development]] that enables task performance currently beyond independent capability. Six defining functions: recruitment (engaging learner interest in the task), reduction of degrees of freedom (simplifying the task without eliminating its essential structure), direction maintenance (keeping the learner focused on the goal), marking critical features (highlighting key errors and relevant task features), frustration control (managing the affective response to difficulty), and demonstration (modelling ideal solut...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Scaffolding-—-Instructional-Design|Scaffolding — Instructional Design]]*

> [!definition] **Schema** **[perm-note]**
> In cognitive science, a [[Schema]] is an organized pattern of thought or behavior that organizes information and relationships among information. Schemas act as mental frameworks that help us interpret new information in relation to existing knowledge structures.
> 
> *Source: topic-set-cognitive-science-pkb-achitecture-202511202330* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Accommodation vs. Assimilation** **[perm-note]**
> Assimilation is the incorporation of new information into an existing schema without changing the schema's structure — the new information is interpreted through the existing frame. Accommodation is the modification of an existing schema, or the creation of a new one, in response to information that cannot be assimilated into current structures without distortion. In academic reading, assimilation is the default process and produces efficient comprehension when schemas are accurate; accommodation is the more cognitively demanding process required when texts challenge or contradict existing ...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / reading comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Accommodation-vs.-Assimilation|Schema Accommodation vs. Assimilation]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Activation** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which a particular schema is retrieved from long-term memory and made available in working memory for deployment in ongoing comprehension or performance. Activation is triggered by cues in the environment, spreading through associative networks, raising activation levels of related schemas, with the schema best matching available cues reaching control threshold. See also: [[Spreading-Activation]], [[Schema-Progressive-Architecture]].
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Activation|Schema Activation]]*

> [!definition] **Schema as Variable-Slot Data Structure** **[perm-note]**
> For Rumelhart, a schema is a data structure with two components: a *fixed structure* that defines what the schema is for (the type), and a set of *variable slots* that are instantiated with specific values when the schema is applied to a particular case. The room schema has fixed structure (enclosed space used for human activity) and slots for dimensions, walls, floor, ceiling, doors, furniture, and occupants. Slot values are assigned by incoming data when data are available, or by *default values* — the schema's built-in expectations about the most probable slot contents in the absence of ...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-as-Variable-Slot-Data-Structure|Schema as Variable-Slot Data Structure]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Attractor** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A reconceptualization of schemas as soft stable states (attractors) in a cognitive landscape shaped by statistical learning over embodied, culturally situated experience. Unlike a data structure (discrete, pre-formed, retrieved), an attractor is reconstructed on each instantiation, is sensitive to context (the full constellation of current cues), and emerges from regularization over distributed experience. NOT identical to a connectionist "attractor network" in the technical sense, though informed by that architecture.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The core reconstructiv...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Attractor|Schema Attractor]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Automation** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which frequently activated [[knowledge-schemas]] in long-term memory become faster and less resource-demanding in working memory, eventually retrievable and deployable as single units rather than networks of individually active elements. Schema automation is the mechanism that underlies expertise: as schemas automate, effective element interactivity decreases, freeing working memory capacity for new learning. Schema automation is theorized to be the goal of all instructional schemas — what [[Germane-Load]] is supposed to support.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Automation|Schema Automation]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Automation in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> [[Schema-Automation]] in the reading context refers to the process by which orthographic patterns — from individual grapheme-phoneme correspondences to morphemes, syllables, and whole words — become encoded as unified long-term memory schemas that can be retrieved as wholes rather than constructed from parts. When the spelling pattern "tion" has been encountered sufficient times, its phonological equivalent /ʃən/ can be retrieved as a unit from long-term memory rather than assembled from four separate grapheme-phoneme correspondences. The automated schema bypasses the capacity-limited worki...
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Automation-in-Reading|Schema Automation in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Competition** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which multiple partially-activated schemas compete for dominance in interpreting a given input. Competition is mediated by lateral inhibition: schemas that are mutually inconsistent suppress each other's activation. The schema with the highest total activation (bottom-up input match + top-down contextual facilitation) wins the competition and achieves full instantiation. Schema competition resolution is fast (typically <500ms) and largely automatic; it is experienced as "understanding" a situation without awareness of the competition process.
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Competition|Schema Competition]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which episodic instances (hippocampally-encoded, context-specific) are gradually extracted and integrated into abstract neocortical schema representations. Occurs primarily during slow-wave sleep via hippocampal sharp-wave ripple/neocortical sleep spindle coupling. Produces the [[Schema-Crystallization-Event]] — the threshold crossing at which accumulated instances suddenly manifest as a stable, rapidly-accessible schema rather than a collection of separate episodes.
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Consolidation|Schema Consolidation]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Construction** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The cognitive process by which a new schema is created where no adequate prior schema existed. Distinguished from accommodation (modifying an existing schema) and accretion (adding information to an existing schema). Primary mechanisms: analogical induction from structurally isomorphic source domains, pattern induction from regularities across multiple examples.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** Schema construction is not the same as learning. Most learning involves schema activation (assimilation) or accommodation; schema construction occurs only when input cannot be assimilated or...
> 
> *Attribution: restructuring mode*
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Construction|Schema Construction]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Crystallization Event** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A qualitative threshold event in schema construction at which an accumulating set of loosely organized knowledge elements undergoes rapid structural consolidation into a coherent, retrievable schema. Pre-crystallization, knowledge elements are available but not organized into a single retrievable unit; post-crystallization, the schema can be activated as a whole.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The crystallization event model is central to the analysis of Phase IV. It captures something that smooth, incremental accounts of schema formation miss: the discontinuous, thresho...
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Crystallization-Event|Schema Crystallization Event]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Externalization** **[perm-note]**
> Schema externalization is the practice of building external knowledge structures that mirror the organizational principles of [[Schema-Theory|schematic]] long-term memory — including relational hierarchies, categorical clustering, associative linking, and activation pathway design — such that the external system can perform some of the functions of internal schema-driven processing. Schema externalization is distinguished from mere information storage by its emphasis on structure rather than content: the claim is not just that external systems can hold facts that memory cannot, but that the...
> 
> *Source: schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Externalization|Schema Externalization]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Formation** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive process by which repeated encounters with structurally similar information produce a generalized mental representation encoding the abstract pattern, its internal relationships, and its conditions of application. Schemas reduce the working memory demands of processing familiar content by allowing it to be retrieved as a single unit rather than reconstructed from components.
> 
> **Boundary:** Schema formation is not simple memorisation. A schema for "linked note in Obsidian" is not just the memory that linked notes exist — it is an organized representation of when to create one, h...
> 
> *Source: pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Formation|Schema Formation]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Hierarchy** **[perm-note]**
> A schema hierarchy is the nested organization of schemas whereby higher-level schemas embed lower-level sub-schemas as components. The PHYSICIAN-VISIT schema embeds an EXAMINATION sub-schema, which itself embeds DIAGNOSTIC-REASONING sub-schemas. The RESTAURANT schema embeds ORDER-TAKING, FOOD-DELIVERY, and PAYMENT sub-schemas.
> 
> **Properties of hierarchical embedding:**
> - A sub-schema can appear in multiple parent schemas (PAYMENT appears in both RESTAURANT and RETAIL-PURCHASE)
> - Higher-level schemas provide the *context* (instantiating values) that fill the slots in sub-schemas
> - Schema fai...
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Hierarchy|Schema Hierarchy]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Instantiation** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which an abstract schema receives specific variable values from the current input, producing a specific instance interpretation. Instantiation is NOT simple retrieval: it is a constraint-satisfaction process in which the abstract variable structure is bound to specific values that maximally satisfy the constraints while accounting for the available input. Instantiation involves both value binding (assigning specific values to slots) and inference generation (predicting likely values for slots not directly specified by input).
> 
> **Complexity:** Instantiation can be partial — so...
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[schema-instantiation|Schema Instantiation]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Intrusion Error** **[perm-note]**
> A memory error in which schema-consistent information that was NOT present in the original experience is nonetheless "remembered" as having been present. Caused by the reconstructive nature of memory: when episodic fragments are insufficient for complete reconstruction, schema inference fills the gaps — and the inferences reflect the retriever's schemas, not the original event. Intrusion errors increase with retention interval (as episodic fragments decay and reconstruction increasingly relies on schemas alone).
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Intrusion-Error|Schema Intrusion Error]]*

> [!definition] **Schema — Core Definition** *[needs-note]*
> A **schema** (plural: *schemas* or *schemata*) is a structured, hierarchical, and dynamic cognitive framework that encodes generalized knowledge about a domain, category, event, or situation. Schemas are mental data structures organized around a central concept or prototype that contain variables (slots) with associated ranges of permissible values (default values). Rather than encoding specific episodic facts, schemas represent abstracted, typicalized knowledge derived from experience — the "what typically goes together" structure of the world as the mind has modeled it.
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-—-Core-Definition|Schema — Core Definition]]*

> [!definition] **Schema-Attractor Basin** **[perm-note]**
> In connectionist accounts of schema architecture, the region of network state-space toward which the network dynamics converge when initialized in the vicinity. Each learned schema correspondence to an attractor basin; schema activation is settling into an attractor. Basin depth corresponds to schema strength (stronger schemas have deeper basins, requiring more counter-evidence to exit). Basin overlap corresponds to schema similarity (overlapping basins produce blending and interference).
> 
> **Distinguishing feature:** Unlike symbolic schemas, which are stored explicitly at a known address, a...
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Attractor-Basin|Schema-Attractor Basin]]*

> [!definition] **Schema-Fidelity** **[perm-note]**
> Schema-fidelity refers to the degree to which a [[Personal-Knowledge-Base]]'s organizational structure mirrors the relational, hierarchical, and associative properties of schematic [[Long-Term-Memory]]. High-fidelity PKBs contain richly typed, conceptually meaningful links; hierarchical schema nodes (MOCs, index notes) that constrain connected detail notes; and an associative network structure that supports spreading activation analogues. Schema-fidelity is a spectrum, not a binary, and functions as an explicit integrating design criterion for PKB construction.
> 
> **Boundary:** Schema-fidelit...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Fidelity]]*

> [!definition] **Script Schema** **[perm-note]**
> A **script** (Schank & Abelson, 1977) is a specialized event schema that encodes knowledge about the typical sequence of actions, actors, props, and outcomes in a stereotyped situation. Classic examples include the *restaurant script*, the *doctor's office script*, and the *grocery shopping script*. Scripts are particularly important for understanding narrative text and social situations because they supply the implicit causal connective tissue that texts omit.
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Script-Schema|Script Schema]]*

> [!definition] **SDL as Educational Goal** **[perm-note]**
> The aspiration that learners develop the capacity and inclination to take increasing responsibility for their own learning throughout life. In this sense, SDL names what educators are working toward when they design autonomy-supportive environments, employ learning contracts, or engage in coaching rather than direct instruction. This usage is normative rather than descriptive — it says what education *should* produce, rather than what is happening in a given learning episode.
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[SDL-as-Educational-Goal|SDL as Educational Goal]]*

> [!definition] **The SDT Motivational Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> [**SDT-Continuum**:: (1) Amotivation — absence of intent, neither intrinsically nor extrinsically regulated; (2) External Regulation — behavior driven by external rewards and punishments, fully externally regulated; (3) Introjected Regulation — behavior driven by internal pressures (guilt, shame, contingent self-esteem), partially introjected but still controlling self-regulation; (4) Identified Regulation — behavior driven by personally endorsed values, goals, and importance; (5) Integrated Regulation — behavior whose regulation has been fully synthesized with core values and identity; (6)...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-SDT-Motivational-Continuum|The SDT Motivational Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Secure Base** **[perm-note]**
> In Attachment Theory, the relational foundation provided by a secure attachment figure that enables exploratory behavior. Used in this report as a cross-theoretical parallel to the BPNT mechanism by which relatedness satisfaction (relational security) enables autonomous engagement.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Secure-Base|Secure Base]]*

> [!definition] **Selection** **[perm-note]**
> The first process in Wittrock's Select-Organize-Integrate (SOI) model, referring to the allocation of attentional resources to particular elements of the learning environment. Selection is not passive filtering but an active, top-down constructive process in which the learner's prior knowledge and goals shape what is attended to. Expert learners select more efficiently because they can recognize the most conceptually important elements. See also: [[Attention]], [[Prior-Knowledge-Activation]], [[Selective-Attention]]
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Selection]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Agent Coordinator** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>**Self-Agent Coordinator:**</span> An AI agent operating mode where the agent autonomously manages context loading, session continuity, resource discovery, and task execution within a structured Personal Knowledge Base. The agent acts as both executor and orchestrator, intelligently navigating vault resources without requiring step-by-step user instruction.
> 
> *Source: pkb-report-llm-agent-coordination-system-implemntation-guide-pkb+codebase-scaffold-2025122311* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Agent-Coordinator|Self-Agent Coordinator]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Concordance** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a goal or behavior authentically expresses the person's core values, interests, and developing sense of self — the degree to which it is self-determined in BPNT's sense. Self-concordant goals are associated with greater effort, persistence, well-being, and goal attainment than non-concordant goals even when controlling for goal difficulty. **See also:** [[Goal-Pursuit]], [[Authentic-Self-Expression]], [[Eudaimonic-Well-Being]]
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Concordance]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Concordance Model** **[perm-note]**
> A related SDT framework proposing that goals vary in how concordant they are with the person's authentic values and core self. Self-concordant goals — pursued because they reflect genuine values and interests rather than external pressure — are associated with greater sustained effort, more positive affect during pursuit, and greater well-being benefits upon attainment. The Self-Concordance Model complements GCT by explaining the motivational quality of goal engagement, while GCT explains the content of what is aspired toward.
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Concordance-Model|Self-Concordance Model]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Consistency** **[perm-note]**
> [**Self-Consistency**:: A prompting reliability technique that generates multiple independent [[Chain-of-Thought]] reasoning paths for a single query through high-temperature sampling, then aggregates final answers via [[Majority Voting]] to select the most statistically consistent conclusion, thereby mitigating the stochasticity and potential errors inherent in single-sample LLM generation.]^established
> 
> *Source: prompt-report-self-consistency-complexity-based-consistency-202512250916* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Consistency]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Determination** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which behavior arises from a person's own volition rather than from external compulsion or internal pressure. Self-determination is the overarching dimension that the regulatory continuum tracks — from fully non-self-determined (amotivation, external regulation) through partially self-determined (introjected) to fully self-determined (identified, integrated, intrinsic). Self-determination is the conceptual core of SDT and the measured quantity in OIT research.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Determination]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Determination Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A macro-theory of human motivation and personality developed by **Edward L. Deci** and **Richard M. Ryan**. It asserts that humans are active organisms with innate tendencies toward psychological growth and integration. SDT posits that optimal functioning, well-being, and high-quality motivation depend on the continuous satisfaction of three universal psychological needs: **Autonomy** (the need to feel volition and self-endorsement of one's actions), **Competence** (the need to feel effective in one's interactions with the environment), and **Relatedness** (the need to feel connected to and...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-self-determination-theory-as-a-driver-for-sustainable-knowledge-management-habits-20251126101939* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Determination-Theory|Self-Determination Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Determined Self-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Integrative concept developed in this report. The state in which both motivational quality (autonomous, need-satisfying — SDT level) and regulatory architecture (sophisticated, cyclical, well-calibrated — SRL level) are jointly optimal. Characterised by deliberate practice that is phenomenologically experienced as vitalising rather than depleting. Neither high autonomous motivation nor sophisticated SRL alone produces this state; it requires their integration.
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Determined-Self-Regulation|Self-Determined Self-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Directed Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A process in which individuals take the initiative, with or without the assistance of others, in diagnosing their learning needs, formulating learning goals, identifying human and material resources for learning, choosing and implementing appropriate learning strategies, and evaluating learning outcomes. SDL in this sense is a description of *how* learning is organised and managed — it specifies a set of activities and their sequence, not a personal characteristic. A learner engages in SDL when they perform these activities; they fail to engage in SDL when these activities are performed by ...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Directed-Learning|Self-Directed Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Directed Learning Readiness Scale** **[perm-note]**
> A 58-item psychometric instrument measuring attitudes, abilities, and characteristics associated with SDL readiness across eight factors: openness to learning, self-concept as an effective learner, initiative and independence, informed acceptance of responsibility, love of learning, creativity, future orientation, and basic study and problem-solving skills. Widely used in research and practice for over four decades; subject to ongoing psychometric debate regarding factor structure stability and cross-cultural validity. Most defensible as a general screening tool rather than a diagnostic pro...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Directed-Learning-Readiness-Scale|Self-Directed Learning Readiness Scale]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Directedness — Trait or Readiness Sense** **[perm-note]**
> A relatively stable cluster of attitudes, abilities, and personal characteristics associated with taking initiative and responsibility in learning. This includes openness to learning opportunities, self-concept as an effective and independent learner, initiative and independence in learning, informed acceptance of responsibility for one's own learning, love of learning, creativity, future orientation, and the ability to use basic study and problem-solving skills. Self-directedness, in this sense, is not something one does but something one *has* — a readiness or propensity that varies acros...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Directedness-—-Trait-or-Readiness-Sense|Self-Directedness — Trait or Readiness Sense]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Efficacy** **[perm-note]**
> Belief in one's capacity to perform a specific task in a specific domain. Distinguished from general confidence or general intelligence beliefs by its situational specificity. High self-efficacy is predictive of choice, effort, and persistence in that specific domain. Self-efficacy is primarily built through mastery experiences (actual successes), not through instruction about ability beliefs. The empirical foundation for Axiom 2 of this report.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Efficacy]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Efficacy Belief** **[perm-note]**
> A learner's judgment about their capability to perform the specific actions required to produce a specific outcome in a particular context. Distinguished from global self-concept, self-efficacy is task-specific, contextually sensitive, and empirically one of the strongest motivational predictors of cognitive strategy use and academic achievement in the SRL literature.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Efficacy-Belief|Self-Efficacy Belief]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Efficacy Calibration** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The process by which self-efficacy beliefs are continuously updated through real-time inner speech commentary during learning activity. Distinct from efficacy updating through discrete outcome experiences; operates continuously through verbal persuasion — in this context, self-administered verbal persuasion. Calibration can be flexible (responsive to performance and strategy, not fixed) or brittle (responsive to performance interpreted through ability vocabulary, producing fragility under challenge).
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The Self-Efficacy Calibration Mechanism ...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Efficacy-Calibration|Self-Efficacy Calibration]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Efficacy for Learning and Performance** **[perm-note]**
> A learner's judgment about their capability to perform the cognitive and self-regulatory activities required to achieve academic goals in a specific context. Distinguished from global self-concept by its contextual and task-specificity. The strongest and most consistent motivational predictor of cognitive strategy use in the SRL empirical literature.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Efficacy-for-Learning-and-Performance|Self-Efficacy for Learning and Performance]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Evaluation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The process of comparing one's current performance against internal or external standards to assess progress toward goals. In the SRL framework, self-evaluation should primarily compare performance against the *process goals* and *outcome goals* set during the Forethought Phase — not against abstract standards of excellence or comparisons with other learners.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The quality of self-evaluation in the Self-Reflection Phase is almost entirely determined by the quality of goal-setting in the Forethought Phase. Vague goals produce vague self-evalua...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Evaluation]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Explanation** **[perm-note]**
> A comprehension strategy involving the generation of explanatory verbalizations or written articulations during or after reading that express in the reader's own words what a passage means, how it relates to prior text and knowledge, and what gaps remain in understanding. Self-explanation operationalizes the principle that reformulation in one's own words requires and simultaneously develops genuine comprehension. It is distinguished from summarization (accurate restatement without explanatory elaboration) and from annotation (written marginalia that may or may not involve reformulation).
> 
> ...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[self-explanation]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Explanation Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that learners who generate explanatory text while studying material — articulating the causal mechanisms behind worked examples, definitions, or claims — develop significantly deeper understanding than those who read the same material without generating explanations.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Explanation-Effect|Self-Explanation Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Management** **[perm-note]**
> In Garrison's three-component SDL model, the behavioural dimension of SDL: the learner's organised control of the external learning environment, including time allocation, resource identification and selection, and task structuring. Self-management is necessary but not sufficient for SDL; it must be accompanied by self-monitoring and appropriate motivation. Self-management failures (disorganisation, resource misselection, time management breakdown) are a common proximate cause of SDL failure, though their ultimate causes often lie in motivational quality or metacognitive readiness.
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Management]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Monitoring** **[perm-note]**
> In Garrison's three-component SDL model, the cognitive and metacognitive dimension of SDL: the learner's ongoing assessment of their own understanding, identification of comprehension failures, and deployment of remedial strategies. Self-monitoring requires metacognitive capacity that many learners do not apply spontaneously, particularly in domains where they lack background knowledge. The competence problem in SDL — that novices cannot accurately diagnose their own learning needs or evaluate their own understanding — is precisely a self-monitoring failure that must be addressed through in...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Monitoring]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Monitoring vs. Illusion of Knowing** **[perm-note]**
> Self-monitoring in the cognitive area must be distinguished from the illusion of knowing — the pervasive tendency for learners to misread their own comprehension states as more complete than they actually are. This metacognitive inaccuracy, documented extensively in the metacognitive calibration literature, is one of the central reasons why self-monitoring competence must be explicitly developed rather than assumed. Effective self-monitoring requires not only the motivation to check comprehension but the capacity to reliably distinguish genuine understanding from surface familiarity.
> 
> *Source: pintrich-model-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Monitoring-vs.-Illusion-of-Knowing|Self-Monitoring vs. Illusion of Knowing]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Reference Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The robust empirical finding that encoding information with reference to the self (asking whether information applies to one's own traits or experiences) produces superior free recall compared to impersonal semantic encoding. Attributed to the self-concept functioning as a maximally rich, well-organized associative structure that provides numerous retrieval pathways. Modulated by cultural context: collectivistic cultures may show analogous effects for close in-group others.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Reference-Effect|Self-Reference Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Reflection Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The third phase of the cyclical model, encompassing self-judgment (comparing performance to goals and attributing outcomes causally) and self-reaction (emotional and adaptive/defensive inference responses). The output of this phase feeds directly into the next forethought cycle.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Reflection-Phase|Self-Reflection Phase]]*

> [!definition] **The Self-Reflection Phase** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition:** The third phase of [[Zimmerman's-Cyclical-SRL-Model|Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model]], occurring after a learning activity. It encompasses two interacting sub-components: **Self-Judgment** (self-evaluation against a standard, and causal attribution for outcomes) and **Self-Reaction** (self-satisfaction or dissatisfaction, and adaptive or defensive inferences about future learning behavior). The Self-Reflection Phase is the evaluative closure of one cycle and the generative opening of the next.
> **Boundary Conditions:** The Self-Reflection Phase is not the same as reviewing not...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)* | *Domain: self-regulated-learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Self-Reflection-Phase|The Self-Reflection Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Reflection-Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The third phase of Zimmerman's SRL cycle, encompassing Self-Judgement (self-evaluation and causal attribution) and Self-Reactions (adaptive/defensive inferences and self-satisfaction/affect). The Self-Reflection Phase converts performance experience into regulatory adaptation for the next cycle.
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-applied-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Reflection-Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Regulated Learning** **[perm-note]**
> The capacity of learners to monitor, direct, and regulate their own cognitive, motivational, and behavioural processes in service of self-set or internalised learning goals. Self-regulated learners can generate their own feedback through self-monitoring, identify discrepancies between current and desired performance, and select and deploy strategies to close those gaps. The developmental endpoint toward which autonomy-supportive feedback design is aimed.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Regulated-Learning|Self-Regulated Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Regulated Learning / SRL** **[perm-note]**
> The proactive, metacognitively-directed management of one's own cognitive, motivational, behavioural, and contextual processes in service of self-set learning goals. SRL is distinguished from simple learning effort by its cyclical, self-directed quality — learners monitor and regulate their own processes rather than simply responding to external instruction. *Note: SRL frameworks describe the cognitive architecture of regulation; SDT describes the motivational quality of its enactment.*
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Regulated-Learning-SRL|Self-Regulated Learning / SRL]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Regulated Learning — SRL** **[perm-note]**
> An active, constructive process by which learners set goals, monitor progress, and regulate their cognition, motivation, and behavior. SRL is distinguished from passive learning by the learner's agentic role in initiating, sustaining, and adjusting their learning processes. Not synonymous with self-directed learning, which emphasizes the learner's control over goals and context; SRL can occur within externally set goals.
> 
> *Source: zimmerman-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Regulated-Learning-—-SRL|Self-Regulated Learning — SRL]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Regulated Reading** *[needs-note]*
> Reading as a cyclical, goal-directed self-regulatory enterprise in which the reader engages in pre-reading forethought (goal-setting, prior knowledge activation, strategy planning), performance-phase monitoring and control (comprehension monitoring, strategy deployment, attention regulation), and post-reading self-reflection (outcome evaluation, attribution, strategy adaptation). Self-regulated reading is distinguished from externally regulated reading (strategy use in response to teacher prompts alone) and from automatized habitual reading (reading without metacognitive engagement).
> 
> **See...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Regulated-Reading|Self-Regulated Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The active, constructive process by which learners set goals and monitor, regulate, and control their cognition, motivation, and behavior, guided by goals and environmental features. Encompasses four areas (cognitive, motivational/affective, behavioral, contextual) and four phases (forethought, monitoring, control, reflection).
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Serving Attribution Bias** **[perm-note]**
> The **self-serving attribution bias** is the motivationally driven tendency to attribute success to internal, personal factors (I am skilled, I worked hard, I had a good plan) and failure to external, situational factors (the task was unfair, I had bad luck, circumstances conspired against me). This asymmetry in blame assignment vs. credit-taking serves self-enhancing and self-protective functions: it maximizes pride from success while minimizing shame from failure. The bias is most pronounced when both self-concept and social presentation are at stake, and it can be reduced or even reverse...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Serving-Attribution-Bias|Self-Serving Attribution Bias]]*

> [!definition] **Semantic Density** **[perm-note]**
> In PKM architecture, the degree to which notes encode not only information but the relationships, implications, distinctions, and contexts that make information cognitively useful without additional working memory reconstruction. A semantically dense note allows immediate resumption of thinking from where it left off; a semantically sparse note (e.g., a bare quote or title) requires the practitioner to reconstruct relational structure internally before the note can function as a cognitive resource.
> 
> **Boundary:** Semantic density is not equivalent to length or complexity. A short, precisely...
> 
> *Source: pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[Semantic-Density|Semantic Density]]*

> [!definition] **Semantic Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The subsystem of declarative memory responsible for general knowledge about the world — facts, concepts, word meanings, encyclopaedic knowledge — divorced from the specific episodes in which they were acquired. When you know that Paris is the capital of France, you typically do not remember the specific occasion on which you learned this; the episodic context has been lost while the semantic content persists. Semantic memory is relatively resistant to the organic amnesias that devastate episodic memory, suggesting some neurological independence between the two systems.
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
